DCGI 批准 QDENGA 登革热 疫苗 在印度上市 - The News Mill

DCGI grants approval for QDENGA dengue vaccine in India - The News Mill
新闻简报印度已批准 QDENGA,这是其首款登革热 疫苗,用于 4-60 岁的人群,标志着在对抗这种占全球负担近三分之一的疾病方面取得了重大进展。到 2025 年,报告病例将超过 113,000 例,模型显示还会有数千万例病例,但由于流行、城市化和气候因素,登革热仍然是全年的威胁。根据广泛的临床试验,经 DCGI 批准的 疫苗 对确诊的登革热有 80.2% 的疗效,对住院治疗有 90.4% 的疗效。武田将与印度当局合作,以世界卫生组织的建议和资格预审为基础,确保可及性。此次批准与 媒介 控制和 监测 一起加强了印度全面的登革热预防战略。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表于2026-07-29;证据中未指定事件日期。
地点与对象
印度;4-60岁的个人。
事件
已确认:印度药品监管总局 (DCGI) 授予 QDENGA 登革热 疫苗 的市场授权,这是印度首个获得批准的产品。
规模、影响与风险
印度占全球登革热负担的近三分之一;到 2025 年,报告的病例数将超过 113,000 例,模型显示每年实际感染人数为数千万例。由于流行、城市化和气候因素,登革热对全年的公共卫生构成威胁。
应对、状态与不确定性
批准基于全球临床试验(19 项试验,28,000 多名参与者)和 III 期 TIDES 试验(20,000 多名参与者)。QDENGA 在 12 个月时对经病毒学证实的登革热有 80.2% 的疗效,在 18 个月时对住院治疗的疗效为 90.4%。武田将与印度当局合作进行规划和交付。世卫组织推荐 QDENGA 并对其在流行环境中进行资格预审。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

DCGI grants approval for QDENGA dengue vaccine in India - The News Mill

News Brief
India has approved QDENGA, its first dengue vaccine, for individuals aged 4-60, marking a significant advancement in combating a disease that accounts for nearly one-third of the global burden. With over 113,000 reported cases in 2025 and modelling suggesting tens of millions more, dengue remains a year-round threat due to endemicity, urbanisation, and climate factors. The vaccine, approved by the DCGI, demonstrates 80.2% efficacy against confirmed dengue and 90.4% against hospitalisation, based on extensive clinical trials. Takeda will collaborate with Indian authorities to ensure accessibility, building on WHO recommendations and prequalification. This approval strengthens India's comprehensive dengue prevention strategy alongside vector control and surveillance.
Original source text (English)
Takeda Biopharmaceuticals India Pvt Ltd has announced that the Drug Controller General of India (DCGI) has granted market authorisation for QDENGA, Takeda’s dengue vaccine, for individuals aged 4-60. This approval represents the first dengue vaccine authorised in India and permits use regardless of previous dengue exposure, without requiring pre-vaccination testing. Discover more public health Health Public Health Dengue, a mosquito-borne viral disease prevalent in over 125 countries, continues to pose a major public health challenge. India accounts for nearly one-third of the global dengue burden, with reported cases exceeding 113,000 in 2025. However, modelling analyses suggest that actual infections likely number in the tens of millions annually. Dr Mahender Nayak, Head of Intercontinental Markets at Takeda, stated, “Dengue is a growing public health challenge, and India needs sustained, evidence-based prevention. The latest seven-year data for QDENGA show continued protection against dengue infection and hospitalisation across all four serotypes; an important milestone for communities and health systems. Since its launch in 2022, QDENGA has been approved in 43 countries, with more than 32 million doses distributed globally. This approval marks an important step forward in strengthening dengue prevention in India. Building on Takeda’s 245-year heritage and more than 70 years of vaccine expertise, we remain committed to working with public health authorities, healthcare professionals, and partners to support equitable and sustainable access.” The widespread endemicity of dengue in India, combined with rapid urbanisation and climate-related factors that promote mosquito proliferation, has made dengue a year-round public health threat. Seasonal surges continue to tax healthcare systems. The disease is complex to manage as it lacks a specific treatment and can rapidly progress to severe, life-threatening complications requiring hospitalisation and intensive care. Discover more infections Company News Biological Sciences Peter Streibl, General Manager of the Southeast Asia and India Cluster at Takeda, commented, “Dengue is a shared regional challenge that requires collaboration across borders and sectors. Takeda is committed to working with governments, public health authorities, healthcare professionals, and partners across South and Southeast Asia, not only to support access to QDENGA, where approved and recommended, but also to strengthen broader dengue prevention efforts, including surveillance, awareness, vector control, and community engagement. Our aim is to contribute to sustainable, evidence-based solutions that reflect the needs of each country.” Dr Goh Choo Beng, Medical Affairs Head Southeast Asia & India Cluster, added, “India carries a substantial dengue burden, and all four dengue virus serotypes have been documented as co-circulating in several regions. QDENGA is designed to protect against all four serotypes, regardless of prior exposure. Its approval represents an important step in strengthening India’s comprehensive approach to dengue prevention, alongside vector control, surveillance, community awareness and other public health measures.” Discover more vaccine Government Agencies Drugs & Medications The Indian approval is based on results from Takeda’s global clinical development programme, which included 19 Phase 1, 2 and 3 clinical trials involving over 28,000 participants in both endemic and non-endemic regions. This programme encompassed the pivotal Phase III TIDES (DEN-301) trial, conducted with over 20,000 participants across eight countries over an extended period to evaluate the long-term safety and efficacy of QDENGA. At 12 months after the second dose, the trial demonstrated an overall vaccine efficacy of 80.2% against virologically confirmed dengue (VCD), meeting its primary endpoint. At 18 months, the study showed 90.4% efficacy against dengue-related hospitalisation, satisfying a key secondary endpoint. After 4.5 years, two doses of QDENGA provided 84.1% efficacy in preventing dengue-related hospitalisations. The vaccine has shown sustained safety and efficacy for up to seven years across all four dengue virus serotypes. Supporting data include a Phase III clinical trial (DEN-302) conducted in Indian participants, which evaluated safety and immunogenicity in individuals aged 4-60 years. This study found QDENGA to be tolerated, safe and immunogenic in healthy adults, adolescents and children. Real-world evidence generation and ongoing pharmacovigilance continue to enhance understanding of the vaccine’s safety and impact. QDENGA is a tetravalent, live-attenuated vaccine designed to protect against all four dengue virus serotypes. It is administered subcutaneously in a two-dose regimen of 0.5 mL each, with doses given three months apart. The World Health Organization (WHO) recommends QDENGA for use in dengue-endemic settings and supports its inclusion in public immunisation programmes in areas with high transmission, without the necessity for pre-vaccination screening. WHO prequalification confirms that the vaccine meets global standards for quality, safety and efficacy, enabling procurement through international agencies such as UNICEF and PAHO to support broader access. QDENGA has been approved in 43 countries across Asia, Latin America and Europe, including India, with more than 32 million doses distributed worldwide via public and private programmes. It is part of Brazil’s National Immunisation Program and is available through public programmes in Argentina, Colombia and Indonesia. Following DCGI’s approval, Takeda will continue collaboration with Indian regulatory authorities, public health stakeholders and healthcare providers to support the planning, delivery and availability of QDENGA in India

为什么印度首例登革热 疫苗 标志着公共卫生里程碑 - ET HealthWorld

Why India's First Dengue Vaccine Marks a Public Health Milestone - ET HealthWorld
新闻简报印度已批准其首个登革热 疫苗 Qdenga,标志着一个重要的公共卫生里程碑。疫苗 由武田开发,针对所有四种登革热血清型,并被批准用于 4 至 60 岁的个体。随着印度报告的登革热病例在 20 年来增加 11 倍,到 2025 年将超过 113,000 例,疫苗 预计将显着减少严重疾病、住院率 80-90% 和死亡人数。医生强调,疫苗接种 必须补充而不是取代现有的蚊虫控制和早期的 治疗 措施。疫苗 预计将于 2027 年初上市,并计划进行公私合作以确保广泛使用。

发布于 2026-07-28。活动日期未明确说明,但最近发生了 疫苗 批准。

印度,特别提到孟买和其他城市地区。受影响人群包括 4 岁至 60 岁的人群,全国各地登革热病例不断上升。
事件
印度中央药品标准控制组织 (CDSCO) 批准了 Qdenga,这是该国首个登革热 疫苗,由武田 (Takeda) 开发。这标志着印度抗击登革热公共卫生战略的一个重要里程碑。
规模、影响与风险
2025 年,印度报告的登革热病例超过 113,000 例,是过去 20 年来的 11 倍。据估计,真正的负担是每年数千万例感染。疫苗 预计将减少严重疾病、住院率 80-90% 和死亡人数,但它不会消除登革热。
应对、状态与不确定性
疫苗预计将于2027年初进入印度市场。医生强调,疫苗接种必须补充蚊虫控制措施和早期医疗护理。定价和商业可用性尚未公布。政府和私营部门之间的合作被认为对于广泛获取至关重要。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Why India's First Dengue Vaccine Marks a Public Health Milestone - ET HealthWorld

News Brief
India has approved Qdenga, its first dengue vaccine, marking a major public health milestone. Developed by Takeda, the vaccine targets all four dengue serotypes and is approved for individuals aged 4 to 60. With reported dengue cases in India rising 11-fold over two decades to over 113,000 in 2025, the vaccine is expected to significantly reduce severe disease, hospitalizations by 80-90%, and deaths. Doctors stress that vaccination must complement, not replace, existing mosquito control and early treatment measures. The vaccine is anticipated to reach the market by early 2027, with plans for public-private collaboration to ensure widespread accessibility.
Original source text (English)
Why India's First Dengue Vaccine Marks a Public Health Milestone As climate change, rapid urbanisation and population growth fuel the spread of dengue, CDSCO's approval of Takeda's Qdenga marks a significant step in India's disease prevention strategy. With reported dengue cases rising 11-fold over the past two decades, doctors say the country's first dengue vaccine could reduce severe disease, hospitalisations and deaths, while calling for its eventual inclusion in the National Immunisation Programme. - Exclusive It's free content, simply login/signup to unlock Get in-depth Industry Insights and Analysis through our “Exclusive” content, presented to you by our esteemed panel of writers, for free By continuing, you agree to the T&C, Privacy Policy and Prohibited Content Policy. This same account can be used across all ET B2B portals. Mumbai: As dengue continues to claim lives and strain healthcare systems across India every monsoon, the country has taken a significant step towards strengthening its fight against the mosquito-borne disease. The Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO) has approved Qdenga, India's first dengue vaccine, marking a major milestone in the country's efforts to prevent one of its fastest-growing vector-borne diseases.The approval comes at a time when the reported burden of dengue in India has risen sharply. Reported dengue cases have increased 11-fold over the past two decades, according to the study Dengue in India: Temporal and Spatial Expansion in Last Two Decades by the Society of Medical Arthropodology (SOMA). In 2025, reported dengue cases exceeded 113,000, according to data from the National Centre for Vector Borne Diseases Control (NCVBDC). However, researchers believe the true burden is far higher, with modelling studies estimating that India records tens of millions of dengue infections each year.Developed by Japanese biopharmaceutical firm Takeda, the vaccine can be administered to individuals aged 4 to 60 years, including those who have previously been exposed to dengue. The vaccine is expected to reach the Indian market by early 2027 and is designed to induce a strong immune response by generating detectable antibodies against all four dengue virus serotypes—DENV-1, DENV-2, DENV-3 and DENV-4. ETHealthworld spoke to doctors to understand how this approval could reshape dengue prevention and why it represents a landmark moment in India's public-health journey. According to the study, moderate rainfall during the summer monsoon is associated with higher dengue mortality, while heavy rainfall can temporarily reduce transmission through a flushing effect. Warm temperatures above 27°C and humidity levels between 60 per cent and 78 per cent further elevate the risk of dengue. Dr. Prachi Sankhe, Consultant, Internal Medicine Physician, Apollo Hospitals Navi Mumbai, said, "Urbanisation, changing rainfall patterns and increasing population density have all contributed to the growing burden of dengue across many parts of India."She added, "For hospitals, the impact is most visible during seasonal surges, when large numbers of patients present with fever requiring evaluation and monitoring. Those who develop warning signs or severe dengue often require admission and intensive supportive care." Against this backdrop, Dr Vasant Nagvekar, Director, Infectious Diseases, Sir HN Reliance Foundation Hospital, Mumbai, described the approval as a long-awaited development. "It's very painful to see people suffering from a mosquito-borne disease that is entirely preventable. India continues to bear one of the world's highest dengue burdens, with the disease now both epidemic and endemic. The availability of this vaccine is a major step forward because dengue continues to cause severe illness, hospitalisation and avoidable deaths," Dr Nagvekar added. Dr Nagvekar said, "I expect the vaccine to cut hospitalisations by nearly 80 per cent to 90 per cent. Even if people develop dengue after vaccination, the illness is expected to be much milder—similar to how influenza vaccines reduce disease severity. Fewer hospitalisations will naturally translate into lower mortality. If adopted widely through a mass immunisation programme, the vaccine could also contribute to herd immunity." Taking an intensivist lens, Dr Sanjith Saseedharan, Director, Critical Care, S. L. Raheja Hospital, Mahim, "I have seen dengue progress rapidly from what appears to be a mild fever to shock, bleeding and multi-organ failure. Even a meaningful reduction in severe dengue could save lives, ease pressure on hospital wards and intensive care units, and reduce the financial and emotional burden on families." However, doctors emphasised that vaccination alone will not eliminate dengue. Dr Saseedharan said, "The approval represents a shift in India's dengue strategy—from reacting to outbreaks to preventing infections and complications. However, vaccination must complement, not replace, mosquito control measures, early recognition of warning signs and timely medical care." The company is yet to announce pricing and commercial availability. Meanwhile doctors believe close collaboration between the government and the private sector will be essential to maximise the vaccine's impact. "Ideally, the vaccine should be rolled out through both the public and private sectors so that it becomes widely accessible to people across the country," Dr Nagvekar said. In 2025, reported dengue cases exceeded 113,000, according to data from the National Centre for Vector Borne Diseases Control (NCVBDC). However, researchers believe the true burden is far higher, with modelling studies estimating that India records tens of millions of dengue infections each year.Developed by Japanese biopharmaceutical firm Takeda, the vaccine can be administered to individuals aged 4 to 60 years, including those who have previously been exposed to dengue. The vaccine is expected to reach the Indian market by early 2027 and is designed to induce a strong immune response by generating detectable antibodies against all four dengue virus serotypes—DENV-1, DENV-2, DENV-3 and DENV-4. ETHealthworld spoke to doctors to understand how this approval could reshape dengue prevention and why it represents a landmark moment in India's public-health journey. A milestone decision A 2025 study led by researchers from the Indian Institute of Tropical Meteorology, titled Dengue Dynamics, Predictions, and Future Increase Under Changing Monsoon Climate in India, found that changing rainfall patterns and rising temperatures are likely to increase dengue transmission across the country.According to the study, moderate rainfall during the summer monsoon is associated with higher dengue mortality, while heavy rainfall can temporarily reduce transmission through a flushing effect. Warm temperatures above 27°C and humidity levels between 60 per cent and 78 per cent further elevate the risk of dengue. Dr. Prachi Sankhe, Consultant, Internal Medicine Physician, Apollo Hospitals Navi Mumbai, said, "Urbanisation, changing rainfall patterns and increasing population density have all contributed to the growing burden of dengue across many parts of India."She added, "For hospitals, the impact is most visible during seasonal surges, when large numbers of patients present with fever requiring evaluation and monitoring. Those who develop warning signs or severe dengue often require admission and intensive supportive care." Against this backdrop, Dr Vasant Nagvekar, Director, Infectious Diseases, Sir HN Reliance Foundation Hospital, Mumbai, described the approval as a long-awaited development. "It's very painful to see people suffering from a mosquito-borne disease that is entirely preventable. India continues to bear one of the world's highest dengue burdens, with the disease now both epidemic and endemic. The availability of this vaccine is a major step forward because dengue continues to cause severe illness, hospitalisation and avoidable deaths," Dr Nagvekar added. Reducing hospitalisations and severe disease Doctors believe the most immediate public-health impact of vaccination will be a reduction in severe disease, hospitalisations and, ultimately, dengue-related deaths.Dr Nagvekar said, "I expect the vaccine to cut hospitalisations by nearly 80 per cent to 90 per cent. Even if people develop dengue after vaccination, the illness is expected to be much milder—similar to how influenza vaccines reduce disease severity. Fewer hospitalisations will naturally translate into lower mortality. If adopted widely through a mass immunisation programme, the vaccine could also contribute to herd immunity." Taking an intensivist lens, Dr Sanjith Saseedharan, Director, Critical Care, S. L. Raheja Hospital, Mahim, "I have seen dengue progress rapidly from what appears to be a mild fever to shock, bleeding and multi-organ failure. Even a meaningful reduction in severe dengue could save lives, ease pressure on hospital wards and intensive care units, and reduce the financial and emotional burden on families." However, doctors emphasised that vaccination alone will not eliminate dengue. Dr Saseedharan said, "The approval represents a shift in India's dengue strategy—from reacting to outbreaks to preventing infections and complications. However, vaccination must complement, not replace, mosquito control measures, early recognition of warning signs and timely medical care." The road ahead Takeda said the vaccine is expected to become available through private healthcare facilities by early 2027. Asked about discussions with the government on wider deployment, Peter Streibl, General Manager, Southeast Asia and India Cluster, Takeda, told ETHealthworld, "Takeda's goal is to provide broad access to all eligible individuals through inclusion in National Immunisation Programmes (NIPs). However, wider availability through public health systems will depend on independent scientific and policy decisions taken by health authorities."The company is yet to announce pricing and commercial availability. Meanwhile doctors believe close collaboration between the government and the private sector will be essential to maximise the vaccine's impact. "Ideally, the vaccine should be rolled out through both the public and private sectors so that it becomes widely accessible to people across the country," Dr Nagvekar said. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

研究发现,随着多种病毒株一起传播,印度的登革热负担变得更加复杂

India’s dengue burden grows more complex as multiple virus strains circulate together, study finds
新闻简报ICMR-VRDL 网络进行的为期两年的全国 监测 研究表明,由于多种病毒株同时在全国范围内传播,印度正面临着更加复杂的登革热疫情。该研究分析了来自 25 个州和联合领地的 6,889 个实验室确认样本,发现所有四种登革热血清型都在共同传播,其中 DENV-2 占主导地位。大约7%的患者同时感染多种血清型,这与更严重的疾病表现相关,包括血小板计数低和出血并发症。这种高度流行的 传播 对未来的登革热 疫苗接种 计划提出了挑战,因为 疫苗 的有效性因不同菌株而异。研究人员强调需要持续实施 监测 并将登革热 疫苗 纳入公共卫生计划,特别是在流行地区。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布时间 - 2026年7月28日 07:28 pm IST。
地点与对象
印度,横跨 25 个邦和中央直辖区。
事件
印度医学研究局-病毒性疾病诊断实验室网络进行的一项为期两年的全国性监测研究发现,该国多个地区同时存在所有四种登革病毒血清型,几乎每14名患者中就有1人同时感染了多种血清型。
规模、影响与风险
该研究分析了 6,889 个实验室确认的登革热样本。DENV-2 仍然是主要血清型,占所有感染的一半以上。大约 7% 的患者同时感染两种或多种登革热血清型。并发感染的患者更有可能出现严重的登革热症状,包括危险的低血小板计数、出血并发症和严重的关节疼痛。
应对、状态与不确定性
研究结果需要通过长期临床研究来证实。印度目前的全民免疫计划中没有登革热 疫苗,尽管本土 疫苗 候选药物正处于临床开发的后期阶段,并且国际批准的 疫苗 最近获得了在印度进口和销售的监管许可。循环血清型的连续 监测 至关重要,因为 疫苗 的有效性针对不同的登革热毒株有所不同。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

India’s dengue burden grows more complex as multiple virus strains circulate together, study finds

News Brief
India is facing a more complex dengue epidemic as multiple virus strains circulate simultaneously across the country, according to a two-year nationwide surveillance study by the ICMR-VRDL network. The study analyzed 6,889 laboratory-confirmed samples from 25 states and union territories, finding that all four dengue serotypes are co-circulating, with DENV-2 being predominant. Approximately 7% of patients were infected with multiple serotypes concurrently, which was associated with more severe disease manifestations including low platelet counts and bleeding complications. This hyperendemic transmission poses challenges for future dengue vaccination programs, as vaccine effectiveness varies across different strains. Researchers emphasize the need for continuous surveillance and incorporating dengue vaccines into public health programs, particularly in endemic areas.
Original source text (English)

India World Sport Data Health Opinion Science Entertainment Premium Videos Podcast Photos Visual Stories Specials TH Explains eBooks TH Games Newsletter Lit For Life The Huddle Home Sci-Tech Health India’s dengue burden grows more complex as multiple virus strains circulate together, study finds The two-year nationwide surveillance study by the ICMR-VRDL network reveals increased risk of severe disease, and fresh challenges for the rollout of future dengue vaccination programmes Published - July 28, 2026 07:28 pm IST - NEW DELHIBindu Shajan PerappadanREAD LATER SEE ALL Remove There are four dengue serotypes (DENV-1 to 4) and severity depends more on secondary infection than serotype, although DENV-2 has often been linked to severe outbreaks. File image for representation only | Photo Credit: Sandeep Saxena India may be entering a more perilous phase in its dengue epidemic, with multiple strains of the virus now circulating simultaneously across much of the country, raising concerns over an increased risk of severe disease, and posing fresh challenges for the rollout of future dengue vaccination programmes.A two-year nationwide surveillance study by the Indian Council of Medical Research’s (ICMR) Virus Research and Diagnostic Laboratory (VRDL) network found that all four dengue virus serotypes are circulating together in several parts of the country, with nearly one in 14 patients infected with more than one serotype at the same time. India’s first dengue vaccine arrives with a crucial safety caveatThe study, titled ‘Pan-India Molecular Surveillance of Dengue Serotypes, 2023–2025’, analysed 6,889 laboratory-confirmed dengue samples from 45 laboratories across 25 States and Union Territories and found that DENV-2 remained the predominant serotype, accounting for over half of all infections. However, researchers said the broader concern was the widespread co-circulation of multiple serotypes, indicating dengue hyper-endemicity.Eight States and Union Territories reported the presence of all four serotypes, while 15 reported three circulating simultaneously. Around 7% of patients were infected with two or more dengue serotypes concurrently. QDENGA: Can the new Japanese vaccine end the dengue menace in India? | In Focus PodcastThe study also found that patients with concurrent infections were more likely to develop severe manifestations of dengue, including dangerously low platelet counts, bleeding complications and severe joint pain, compared with those infected with a single serotype. While the findings need confirmation through long-term clinical studies, they suggest that simultaneous infection with multiple strains could worsen disease severity.There are four dengue serotypes (DENV-1 to 4) and severity depends more on secondary infection than serotype, although DENV-2 has often been linked to severe outbreaks. The emergence of hyperendemic transmission has implications beyond clinical care, the report notes. India currently has no dengue vaccine in its Universal Immunisation Programme, although indigenous vaccine candidates are in advanced stages of clinical development, and an internationally approved vaccine recently received regulatory clearance for import and sale in India. Researchers look at biomarkers which can predict dengue severityContinuous surveillance of circulating serotypes would be critical because vaccine effectiveness varies against different dengue strains, and shifts in the dominant serotype could influence both outbreak intensity and vaccine performance, researchers said.“Apart from routine steps taken to prevent mosquito breeding, there is certainly a need to incorporate the dengue vaccine into public health programmes, initially targeting endemic areas,” Gautam Menon, Professor of Physics and Biology, Ashoka University, said, speaking about the need for continuous surveillance. “But we also need more regular studies that can alert us about the magnitude of the problem, identify hotspots, and provide early warnings on how serotypes are shifting. This should be an ongoing, regularly reported study,” he added. A two-year nationwide surveillance study by the ICMR-VRDL network found that all four dengue virus serotypes are circulating together in several parts of the country, with nearly one in 14 patients infected with more than one serotype at the same time.
Scale, impact and risk
The study analysed 6,889 laboratory-confirmed dengue samples. DENV-2 remained the predominant serotype, accounting for over half of all infections. Around 7% of patients were infected with two or more dengue serotypes concurrently. Patients with concurrent infections were more likely to develop severe manifestations of dengue, including dangerously low platelet counts, bleeding complications and severe joint pain.
Response, status and uncertainty
The findings need confirmation through long-term clinical studies. India currently has no dengue vaccine in its Universal Immunisation Programme, although indigenous vaccine candidates are in advanced stages of clinical development, and an internationally approved vaccine recently received regulatory clearance for import and sale in India. Continuous surveillance of circulating serotypes would be critical because vaccine effectiveness varies against different dengue strains.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue surge raises concerns as DENV-3 becomes dominant in Malaysia - newswav.com

News Brief
Malaysia is experiencing a significant dengue surge with DENV-3 now the dominant strain, according to the Health Ministry. As of July 23, 48,647 cases have been recorded nationwide, a 49.9% increase from last year, with deaths rising sharply to 36. Dr. Koh Kar Chai of DPAM highlights that most Malaysians have limited prior exposure to DENV-3, increasing susceptibility and the risk of severe dengue, particularly in those previously infected with other serotypes. The Klang Valley is most affected, with Selangor reporting over 19,300 cases. Authorities are intensifying surveillance and prevention efforts, urging the public to eliminate mosquito breeding sites and seek prompt medical attention for symptoms.
Original source text (English)
Dengue surge raises concerns as DENV-3 becomes dominant in Malaysia Local Health & Fitness 25 Jul 2026 • 8:22 AM MYT Twentytwo13 Twentytwo13 brings you insights on issues that matter to the people. PETALING JAYA: Dr Koh Kar Chai, Public Education and Advocacy Lead of Dengue Prevention Advocacy Malaysia (DPAM), says the emergence of DENV-3 as the dominant circulating dengue strain is a significant development, as most Malaysians have had limited exposure to it. As of July 23, the Health Ministry recorded 48,647dengue cases nationwide, a 49.9 per cent increase compared with the same period last year. Dengue-related deaths also rose sharply by 89,47 per cent, from 19 to 36. “DENV-3 is currently the dominant dengue strain in the country, unlike previous years when DENV-1 and DENV-2 were the more common circulating serotypes,” Dr Koh told Twentytwo13. “It is significant because most people have not been exposed to this strain before, which could lead to a sharp increase in dengue infections.” Although DENV-3 is not inherently more infectious than the other dengue serotypes, Dr Koh said the shift raises public health concerns because many Malaysians previously infected with DENV-1 or DENV-2 remain susceptible to DENV-3. “People who were previously infected with either DENV-1 or DENV-2 remain susceptible to DENV-3. If they contract a secondary infection, they are at greater risk of developing severe dengue due to what we call antibody-dependent enhancement,” he said. “Many people are unaware that there are four dengue serotypes, meaning a person can contract dengue up to four times in their lifetime. “There is a common misconception that a person who has had dengue once is immune for life. This belief must be dispelled, as a subsequent infection with a different serotype carries a higher risk of severe dengue.” The Health Ministry attributed the rise in cases to several factors, including dengue’s cyclical transmission pattern, favourable weather conditions, rapid urbanisation, persistent mosquito breeding sites and the emergence of DENV-3 as the dominant circulating serotype. The Klang Valley continues to bear the greatest burden of infections, with Selangor recording more than 19,300 cases, followed by Kuala Lumpur and Putrajaya with almost 8,000. Higher case numbers have also been reported in Johor, Sabah, Negeri Sembilan, Perak, Sarawak, Perlis, Terengganu and Labuan. Dr Koh said the emergence of DENV-3 does not fundamentally change existing dengue prevention strategies, but should serve as a timely reminder for Malaysians to strengthen preventive efforts. “The core messages of current dengue prevention campaigns remain the same, but the emergence of DENV-3 should serve as an impetus to intensify these campaigns and alert the public that we may face an increase in severe dengue cases,” he said. The Health Ministry likewise stressed that although the outbreak follows Malaysia’s familiar cyclical pattern, all stakeholders must remain vigilant. The ministry continues to strengthen surveillance, vector control, community engagement and predictive monitoring using climate and geospatial data to identify high-risk areas. Children remain among the groups most vulnerable to severe dengue, particularly if they have previously been infected with a different serotype. “Children are definitely at greater risk of severe dengue if infected with DENV-3, especially if they have had a previous dengue infection,” Dr Koh said. “But this risk is not directly related to DENV-3 itself. Rather, it is because they have previously been infected with a different dengue strain. “Parents are advised to seek medical attention promptly if their child develops a high fever to ensure a proper diagnosis and avoid possible dengue-related complications.” He urged the public to watch for warning signs of severe dengue, including persistent vomiting, severe abdominal pain, bleeding, extreme lethargy, rapid breathing and restlessness, all of which require immediate medical attention. Despite the sharp increase in cases and deaths, Dr Koh said the focus should remain on prevention rather than fear. “There are various factors driving the increase in both infections and mortality,” he said. “The public should recognise that dengue is an endemic disease that can be fatal, and actively adopt the preventive measures promoted through various dengue awareness campaigns, including vaccination.” The Health Ministry has urged Malaysians to conduct weekly inspections to eliminate stagnant water, participate in community clean-up activities and seek prompt medical treatment if dengue symptoms develop. The ministry stressed that sustained public participation remains one of the country’s strongest defences against the disease. View Original Article 209 20 What Do Netizens Say? 抗病毒药物 在登革热预防中的潜力和 治疗 - Contagion Live

The Potential for Antivirals in Dengue Prophylaxis and Treatment - Contagion Live
新闻简报最近的一项 2a 期试验评估了研究中的 抗病毒药物 mosnodenvir 在未接触过登革热的健康成年人中预防登革热的作用。这项在约翰·霍普金斯大学和佛蒙特大学进行的双盲、安慰剂对照研究发现,高剂量莫诺登韦可显着降低 DENV-3 RNA 水平和感染相关症状。该药物表现出剂量依赖性的 抗病毒药物 效应,并且总体耐受性良好,不良事件大多为轻度至中度。尽管取得了这些有希望的结果,强生公司还是停止了莫诺登韦的进一步开发。然而,其他 抗病毒药物 策略,包括诺华的药物和单克隆抗体,仍在进行中,为未来的登革热预防和 抗病毒药物 选择带来了希望。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-07-24。引用的研究进行了 2a 期试验,并于 2025 年发表。
地点与对象
该研究是在约翰霍普金斯大学和佛蒙特大学进行的。参与者是 18 至 55 岁的健康成年人,之前没有接触过登革热,也没有登革热流行史。
事件
一项 2a 期试验在接受 DENV-3 低减毒株攻击的健康参与者中评估了 mosnodenvir(一种研究性 抗病毒药物)的 抗病毒药物 活性和安全性。该试验是一项由强生公司资助的双盲、随机、安慰剂对照研究。
规模、影响与风险
与安慰剂相比,高剂量莫诺登韦与 DENV-3 RNA 水平显着降低相关,显示出剂量依赖性 抗病毒药物 效应。该药物总体耐受性良好,不良事件大多为轻度至中度。然而,小样本量和低减毒 DENV-3 挑战菌株的使用限制了对现实世界环境的普遍适用性。
应对、状态与不确定性
该试验证明了 mosnodenvir 作为 DENV-3 预防选择的可接受的安全性和有前景的 抗病毒药物 活性。然而,强生公司已停止进一步开发莫诺登韦。其他登革热 抗病毒药物 策略,包括诺华的药物和单克隆抗体,仍在开发中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

The Potential for Antivirals in Dengue Prophylaxis and Treatment - Contagion Live

News Brief
A recent phase 2a trial evaluated the investigational antiviral mosnodenvir for dengue prophylaxis in healthy adults without prior dengue exposure. Conducted at Johns Hopkins University and the University of Vermont, the double-blind, placebo-controlled study found that high-dose mosnodenvir significantly reduced DENV-3 RNA levels and infection-related symptoms. The drug showed a dose-dependent antiviral effect and was generally well tolerated, with mostly mild to moderate adverse events. Despite these promising results, Johnson & Johnson has discontinued further development of mosnodenvir. However, other antiviral strategies, including agents from Novartis and a monoclonal antibody, are still in progress, offering hope for future dengue prevention and treatment options.
Original source text (English)
A study found that the investigational antiviral mosnodenvir reduced dengue virus infection RNA levels and symptoms in a controlled setting, supporting its potential as a prophylactic option. Dengue virus infection, transmitted by the Aedes mosquito and caused by 4 distinct serotypes (DENV- 1-4), has an expanding global incidence and geographic range 1 with locally acquired cases to Europe and the United States. 2 While its cases are often mild or asymptomatic, dengue has the potential to cause severe illness, including shock and capillary leak syndrome, in both dengue-naive individuals and those with prior infection. Two vaccines have received worldwide approval for the prevention and long-term protection against reinfection. CYD-TDV (Dengvaxia; Sanofi) is approved for use in certain endemic areas among those aged 9 to 45 years with confirmed prior dengue infection. However, it is not approved for travelers and is being discontinued due to low demand. TAK-003 (Qdenga; Takeda) is approved in many countries for both seropositive and seronegative people. Despite vaccine development and availability, there are no approved antiviral prophylaxis or treatments against dengue, especially in those who are nonimmune. Mosnodenvir is an orally administered small-molecule agent developed to target dengue viral replication. The drug inhibits the de novo formation of the NS4B-NS3 complex. NS4B is a transmembrane protein in the endoplasmic reticulum, which binds NS3 to enhance NS3 helicase activity and increase viral replication. 3 Preclinical studies have shown serotype-independent antiviral activity through in vitro and in vivo models, and recent phase 1 trials have demonstrated an acceptable safety profile. 4 This FIGURE represents the viral replication complex of Dengue virus and the target of mosnodenvir. The NS4B complex is ER (endoplasmic reticulum) bound. 5 Durbin and colleagues conducted a phase 2a trial that assessed the antiviral activity and safety of mosnodenvir in healthy participants who received a challenge virus with a hypoattenuated strain of DENV-3. 6 This was designed as a doubleblind, randomized, placebo-controlled trial at Johns Hopkins University and the University of Vermont, with funding from Johnson & Johnson. Healthy adults aged 18 to 55 years with no prior dengue exposure or endemic travel were enrolled and assigned to mosnodenvir or placebo in a stepwise, dose-escalation design. Participants were randomly assigned 1:1 to receive a 200-mg loading dose of oral mosnodenvir or placebo for the 5 days prior to challenge, and received an inoculation of underattenuated DENV-3 on day 0. The participants were then enrolled 2:1 into a high-dose cohort to establish safety. Further participants were randomly assigned to a high (100 mg), medium (50 mg), or low (10 mg) dose vs placebo in a 3:3:1 ratio for maintenance from day 1 to day 21. Patients were then followed through day 85. Primary outcomes included a reduction in DENV-3 RNA levels, clinical symptoms of infection, serologic response, and safety. The trial was powered to 85% probability to detect a 30% difference in RNA load between groups. High-dose mosnodenvir was associated with a significant reduction in DENV-3 RNA levels compared with placebo, with a clear dose-dependent antiviral effect regarding reduction in RNA levels and dengue infection–related symptoms. Participants with undetectable viral RNA did not have any detectable neutralizing antibodies, suggesting that successful inhibition of viral replication may prevent the development of humoral immunity. Mosnodenvir was generally well tolerated, with mostly mild to moderate adverse events. Only 2 severe events were noted: infection with SARS-CoV-2, and severe elevation in lipase and glucose levels. Together, the findings support mosnodenvir as a promising antiviral for dengue prophylaxis against DENV-3 infection, especially at high doses. However, the small sample size, use of a hypoattenuated DENV-3 challenge strain, and controlled timing of exposure limit generalizability to real-world settings with unpredictable exposure and multiple circulating serotypes. There is further work to be done to study those who have already been infected, those living in endemic regions, and the timing of prophylaxis. Despite these encouraging early results, Johnson & Johnson has discontinued further development of mosnodenvir. 7 However, other dengue antiviral strategies remain in development, including agents that target antiviral replication by Novartis, which are undergoing a phase 2 trial. 8 There is also a monoclonal antibody undergoing evaluation. 9 These show promise for the future of prophylaxis and treatment for dengue virus in the seropositive and seronegative populations. Paper: Durbin AP, Van Wesenbeeck L, Pierce KK, et al. Daily mosnodenvir as dengue prophylaxis in a controlled human infection model. View five news elements
Time
Published on 2026-07-24. The study referenced was conducted with a phase 2a trial and published in 2025.
Location and population
The study was conducted at Johns Hopkins University and the University of Vermont. Participants were healthy adults aged 18 to 55 years with no prior dengue exposure or endemic travel.
Event
A phase 2a trial assessed the antiviral activity and safety of mosnodenvir, an investigational antiviral, in healthy participants challenged with a hypoattenuated strain of DENV-3. The trial was a double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled study funded by Johnson & Johnson.
Scale, impact and risk
High-dose mosnodenvir was associated with a significant reduction in DENV-3 RNA levels compared with placebo, showing a dose-dependent antiviral effect. The drug was generally well tolerated, with mostly mild to moderate adverse events. However, the small sample size and use of a hypoattenuated DENV-3 challenge strain limit generalizability to real-world settings.
Response, status and uncertainty
The trial demonstrated an acceptable safety profile and promising antiviral activity for mosnodenvir as a prophylactic option against DENV-3. However, Johnson & Johnson has discontinued further development of mosnodenvir. Other dengue antiviral strategies, including agents from Novartis and a monoclonal antibody, remain in development.

还没有 暴发:国会大厦警惕登革热 - Philstar.com

No outbreak yet: Capitol on guard against dengue - Philstar.com
新闻简报宿务省于 2026 年 7 月报告了 381 例确诊的登革热病例,卫生官员澄清说,情况尚未达到 暴发 水平,但由于感染人数稳步上升,仍在密切监测中。这种增加归因于雨季,这通常会导致蚊子繁殖增多。托莱多市的病例数最多(49 例),其次是巴里利市(39 例)和塔利萨伊市(34 例)。据报道有两例与登革热相关的死亡。作为回应,该省已在所有国会大厦运营的医院启动登革热快速通道,优先处理疑似病例,并确保及时 治疗。卫生当局强调早期发现和治疗,敦促居民一旦出现症状立即就医。血液供应问题引起了人们的关注,并计划进行捐赠活动以维持充足的库存。目前正在密切监测这种情况以及另一种雨季疾病钩端螺旋体病。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 30 日;该事件发生于 2026 年 7 月,过去三个月病例不断增加。
地点与对象
菲律宾宿务省,包括托莱多市、巴里利、塔里萨伊市、康索拉西翁、卡尔卡市、麦德林、科尔多瓦、达拉盖地、索戈德、达瑙市、阿尔高、巴兰班、孔波斯特拉、利洛安、达安班塔延、莫阿尔博阿尔、旧金山、博尔邦、明拉尼拉、奥斯洛布、巴迪安、博戈市、马德里德霍斯、皮纳蒙加詹等城市和直辖市卡特蒙。受影响人群中,6-10岁儿童是感染率最高的群体,其次是11-20岁人群。
事件
省卫生官员报告称,2026 年 7 月,宿务省确诊了 381 例登革热病例,并澄清说,情况尚未达到 暴发 水平,但由于感染人数稳步上升,正在密切监测。这种增加被描述为雨季期间的典型现象,该省仍处于“戒备”状态,而不是流行病学上的“暴发”。
规模、影响与风险
2026 年 7 月报告了 381 例确诊登革热病例,其中托莱多市病例数最多(49 例),其次是巴里利(39 例)、塔利萨伊市(34 例)、康索拉西翁(33 例)、卡尔卡市和麦德林(各 19 例)。记录了两例与登革热相关的死亡(康索拉西翁和孔波斯特拉各一例)。该省已在所有国会大厦运营的医院启动登革热快速通道,优先处理疑似病例。卫生官员对输血用血液供应有限表示担忧,但指出仍然可以通过血库获得血液。
应对、状态与不确定性
省卫生部门加强了监测并加强了医院的准备工作。国会大厦运营的医院已确保药品、静脉输液、医疗用品和人员的充足库存。登革热快车道已启动,优先评估、检测和管理疑似病例。提醒当地政府部门加强预防措施,定期开展清理工作。计划献血活动是为了解决供应问题。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

No outbreak yet: Capitol on guard against dengue - Philstar.com

News Brief
Cebu Province reported 381 confirmed dengue cases in July 2026, with health officials clarifying that the situation has not reached outbreak level but remains under close monitoring due to a steady rise in infections. The increase is attributed to the rainy season, which typically leads to higher mosquito breeding. Toledo City recorded the highest number of cases (49), followed by Barili (39) and Talisay City (34). Two dengue-related deaths were reported. In response, the province has activated Dengue Fast Lanes in all Capitol-run hospitals to prioritize suspected cases and ensure timely treatment. Health authorities emphasize early detection and treatment, urging residents to seek medical attention promptly if symptoms develop. Blood supply concerns have been raised, with scheduled donation drives to maintain adequate stocks. The situation is being closely monitored alongside leptospirosis, another rainy season disease.
Original source text (English)
Philstar Home The Philippine Star Pilipino Star Ngayon The Freeman CEBU NEWS OPINION METRO CEBU REGION CEBU BUSINESS CEBU SPORTS CEBU LIFESTYLE CEBU ENTERTAINMENT SUPPORT PHILSTAR Pang-Masa Banat Close the sidebar No outbreak yet: Capitol on guard against dengue By Jonnavie Villa(The Freeman) - July 30, 2026 - 12:00am Facebook Twitter CEBU, Philippines — Despite recording 381 confirmed dengue cases across Cebu Province in July, provincial health officials clarified that the situation has not reached outbreak level, although the steady rise in infections has prompted intensified surveillance and heightened hospital preparedness. Capitol Public Health Consultant Dr. Nikki Catalan described the increase as typical during the rainy season but emphasized that the province remains in a "guarded" situation rather than an epidemiological outbreak. Provincial Health Office (PHO) data showed Toledo City recorded the highest number of confirmed dengue cases with 49, followed by Barili with 39, Talisay City with 34, Consolacion with 33, and Carcar City and Medellin with 19 cases each. Cordova and Dalaguete each reported 17 cases, followed by Sogod with 14, Danao City with 13, and Argao, Balamban and Compostela with 12 cases each. Liloan logged 10 cases, while Daanbantayan, Moalboal and San Francisco recorded nine infections each. Borbon, Minglanilla and Oslob registered eight cases each, followed by Badian, Bogo City and Madridejos with seven each, Pinamungajan with five, and Catmon with four. Rainy season spike Dr. Jenina Canoy, PHO chief of hospitals, said the increase in dengue cases coincides with the onset of the rainy season, when mosquito breeding sites become more prevalent. "Ni-spike jud siya nu considering that we're in the rainy season," said Canoy. She said the PHO had already reminded local government units before the rainy season to strengthen dengue prevention measures during coordination meetings. Canoy added that Capitol-run hospitals had prepared in advance by ensuring adequate stocks of medicines, intravenous fluids, medical supplies and sufficient personnel to respond to the anticipated increase in dengue patients. To improve patient management, the provincial government has activated Dengue Fast Lanes in all Capitol-run provincial and district hospitals. Canoy said the initiative enables hospitals to immediately prioritize patients with suspected dengue upon arrival. "Among gi-prepare ang hospitals jud nga ma-kompleto ang supplies necessary, to have this priority lanes for dengue patients so that they can be attended timely," she said. She stressed that activating the fast lanes is part of the province's preparedness strategy and not a response to an outbreak. "In this time amo lang jud gi-activate just to be more prepared with regards to our medicine, supplies, IV fluids, preparation of the staff so that they will be able to cater to patients immediately," Canoy added. Under the system, patients exhibiting signs and symptoms of dengue undergo priority assessment, faster laboratory testing and immediate medical management, allowing health workers to closely monitor cases that could progress into severe dengue. On guard Catalan said dengue cases have continued to rise over the past three months, prompting health authorities to intensify surveillance across the province. "In the past three months ni-saka na jud ang cases," said Catalan. Despite the increase, she emphasized that the number of cases remains below the threshold needed to declare an outbreak. "We'd say guarded. Dili pa matawag nga outbreak but just of concern because of the rise of the cases. Epidemiologically, dili siya outbreak," she said. Instead, Catalan said the province has adopted a more aggressive approach to detecting and treating suspected cases through the Dengue Fast Lanes. "But right now we're more vigilant. Mao ni ang Dengue Fast Lanes. Point being there's a higher or lower tolerance for these kinds of signs and symptoms—deretso na sila didto," she added. Early detection Catalan urged residents not to panic over the increase in cases but to seek medical attention immediately once symptoms develop. "We're really not here to cause panic or fear, but mao lang jud ni ang among gibalik-balik—early detection, early treatment saves lives," she said. She stressed that prompt consultation significantly improves a patient's chances of avoiding severe complications. "The earlier to seek care the better jud. Mao lang na among gi-advocate," Catalan said. Health officials continue to closely monitor dengue alongside leptospirosis, another disease that commonly surges during the rainy season. Earlier PHO monitoring recorded two dengue-related deaths during the current surveillance period—one each from Consolacion and Compostela. Children aged six to 10 years old account for the highest number of infections, followed by individuals aged 11 to 20 years old. Catalan also urged local government units to sustain regular clean-up drives and closely monitor dengue trends within their respective communities to help curb transmission. Blood supply concerns Apart from monitoring cases, Catalan raised concerns over the limited supply of blood products needed by some dengue patients requiring transfusions. She said blood remains available through blood banks but acknowledged that supplies of specific blood components can become scarce. "Nihit jud... In my personal experience because I have a family friend nga nangita jud og dugo," Catalan shared. Canoy said Capitol-run hospitals have scheduled regular mobile blood donation drives every two to three months, depending on each hospital's schedule. She added that the Philippine Red Cross continues to conduct blood donation activities in various cities, municipalities and schools to help maintain adequate blood supplies. Catalan said another bloodletting activity is scheduled on August 4 and 5 as part of the province's wellness caravan to encourage more voluntary blood donors. — /FPL (FREEMAN) SPONSORED ARTICLES SM Cinema Theater formats explained: Your guide to choosing the cinema that best suits you On the Radar July 29, 2026 - 5:25pm GCash brings 'Wais Tindera Caravan' to Cebu, empowering MSMEs with financial tools and responsible borrowing On the Radar July 28, 2026 - 5:00pm GCash brings 'Wais Tindera Caravan' to Cebu, empowering MSMEs with financial tools and responsible borrowing Technology July 28, 2026 - 5:00pm SM Cinema brings FIFA World Cup Final 2026 to the big screen On the Radar July 28, 2026 - 4:38pm Before your first overseas paycheck: 7 financial moves every Filipino professional should make The Budgetarian July 28, 2026 - 4:30pm Elevate everyday living with Acacia Supasleek collection by American Standard Modern Living July 28, 2026 - 3:00pm GCash gathers top global tech leaders shaping the future of AI and cybersecurity at the 5th iGnite Innovation Summit On the Radar July 28, 2026 - 2:02pm 'The Power of X' comes alive at PGDX 2026 On the Radar July 28, 2026 - 2:00pm Bonchon introduces Golden Crunch flavor to complete '7 Ways to Chikin' lineup Food and Leisure July 28, 2026 - 1:55pm Show comments Ad

登革热 和基孔肯雅热等热带疾病在欧洲呈上升趋势 - 连线

Tropical Diseases Like Dengue Fever and Chikungunya Are on the Rise in Europe - WIRED
新闻简报2024年9月,意大利经历了欧洲大陆最大规模的本地登革热暴发,8月中旬至10月中旬,沿海城市法诺报告了199例病例。值得注意的是,这些患者最近都没有去过流行地区,证实了当地的 传播。暴发 强调了在气候变化和白纹伊蚊(亚洲虎蚊)等入侵性蚊子传播的推动下,欧洲热带疾病的威胁日益严重。这些蚊子在温暖的气温下繁衍生息,已在欧洲各地建立种群,预计将向北扩张。专家警告说,即使气温略有升高,也可能导致蚊子全年活动,导致登革热、基孔肯雅热和西尼罗河病毒等地方性疾病流行。虽然欧洲的公共卫生系统迄今为止已经遏制了 监测,但持续进行的 监测 和适应性措施对于减轻未来风险至关重要。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表日期:2026-07-29。事件期间:2024年8月中旬至10月中旬,针对Fano 暴发。
地点与对象
国家:意大利(法诺)。受影响人群:法诺特定社区出现登革热症状的患者。更广泛的背景:欧洲,入侵性蚊子在整个大陆蔓延。
事件
2024年在意大利法诺确认了暴发的本地暴发。暴发涉及199例,是当时欧洲大陆最大的本地登革热暴发。患者没有去过流行地区,表明当地为传播。
规模、影响与风险
病例:2024 年 8 月中旬至 10 月中旬在法诺发现 199 例。传播:法诺一个社区内的本地 传播。风险:由于侵入性蚊子(白纹伊蚊)和气候变化,热带疾病在欧洲持续流行。欧洲也报告了其他蚊媒病毒,如基孔肯雅热和西尼罗河病毒。
应对、状态与不确定性
调查:通过检测确诊登革热病例。回应:在意大利进行蚊子种群控制实验(例如,不育的雄性蚊子)。不确定性:地方性 传播 的未来风险取决于气候条件、蚊子适应和公共卫生系统的准备情况。来源限制:已审查完整的原始文章(content_status:完整)。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Tropical Diseases Like Dengue Fever and Chikungunya Are on the Rise in Europe - WIRED

News Brief
In September 2024, Italy experienced its largest local dengue outbreak in mainland Europe, with 199 cases reported in the coastal city of Fano between mid-August and mid-October. Notably, none of the patients had recently traveled to endemic regions, confirming local transmission. The outbreak highlights the growing threat of tropical diseases in Europe, driven by climate change and the spread of invasive mosquitoes like Aedes albopictus (Asian tiger mosquito). These mosquitoes, which thrive in warmer temperatures, have established populations across Europe and are expected to expand northward. Experts warn that even a slight increase in temperatures could enable year-round mosquito activity and endemic transmission of diseases like dengue, chikungunya, and West Nile virus. While public health systems in Europe have so far contained outbreaks, ongoing surveillance and adaptive measures are critical to mitigate future risks.
Original source text (English)
In September 2024, a handful of patients showed up at the emergency room in the coastal city of Fano, Italy, with mysterious symptoms: a rash on their hands and feet, fever, nausea, and diarrhea. Doctors sent for various tests until one came back with a positive result for dengue, a viral infection spread by mosquitoes, usually in tropical regions of the world. They tested the other patients, and, sure enough, they had dengue too. The strange thing? None of the patients had recently traveled to a country where they might expect to pick up a dengue infection. The only thing they had in common was visiting a particular neighbourhood in Fano. As climate change increases temperatures across Europe, invasive mosquitoes are spreading and introducing disease agents that Europeans would usually think about only when traveling to other continents. The Fano outbreak wasn’t the first time dengue had been detected as spreading locally in mainland Europe, but it was significantly larger than previous incidents, with 199 cases detected between mid-August and mid-October of 2024. Over the past few years, European countries have reported outbreaks of other mosquito-borne viruses such as chikungunya, a painful disease that causes joint swelling, and West Nile virus. In a heating world, so-called tropical diseases are no longer restricted to tropical regions—and researchers expect the trend to continue. The main current culprit is the mosquito Aedes albopictus, commonly known as the Asian tiger mosquito (or just the tiger mosquito, given it’s now found across all continents). Unlike many mosquito species, it bites in the daytime as well as at night. The tiger mosquito already has established populations across Europe, particularly in the south, and we can expect it to move north, says Riccardo Moretti, a senior researcher at the Italian National Agency for New Technologies, Energy and Sustainable Economic Development (ENEA), who has been working on tiger mosquitos for more than 20 years. The tiger mosquito thrives between 20 and 30 degrees Celsius (68 to 86 Fahrenheit), but it can still live at around 10 Celsius (50 Fahrenheit). “This means that at the moment there are already conditions that could allow for the spread of the Asian tiger mosquito, even in England,” Moretti says. At the moment, these conditions are limited to the warmest part of the year in northern European countries. But farther south, such as in Greece, the mosquito season is elongating. Moretti says researchers have found adults buzzing around until December and reappearing already in March; they overwinter as eggs. “There is a concrete risk that in the next few years, we could have a population capable of surviving throughout the year,” Moretti says. At the moment, cases of mosquito-borne diseases in Europe tend to occur toward the end of summer. This is when tiger mosquitoes are most prevalent, and it’s also when more people return from travel. Someone unwittingly brings back an infection that isn’t found in Europe—dengue, say—and gets bitten by a tiger mosquito. The mosquito goes on to bite other people, and the disease spreads locally. But as the weather cools, the mosquitoes get less active and cases subside. If the mosquito season lasts longer, however, more infections can occur. Ultimately, says Moretti, there’s a risk that tropical diseases like dengue could become endemic in parts of Europe, meaning the disease is always there. “It is completely different dealing with occasional cases of virus transmission, and instead dealing with a permanent status of possibility of transmission,” he says. A difference of a couple of degrees can make a big difference. Earlier this year, a study found that the conditions required for tiger mosquitoes to transmit chikungunya were cooler than previously thought. The mosquitoes can spread the virus at just 14 degrees Celsius (57 degrees Fahrenheit) rather than 16 (61 degrees Fahrenheit), meaning outbreaks could occur in most European countries and for longer periods. “That difference of two degrees matters a lot when we are talking about global warming,” says Sandeep Tegar, an epidemiological modeler at the UK Centre for Ecology and Hydrology, who led the study. While warmer temperatures may generally create better conditions for mosquitoes, this isn’t the full story. Too hot—more than 35 degrees Celsius (95 degrees Fahrenheit)—and tiger mosquitoes struggle to survive. Southern France and Spain, impacted by recent heat waves and wildfires, may actually have fewer mosquitoes this year, says Frederic Bartumeus, an ecologist at the Spanish scientific research center CEAB: “It’s not been a great mosquito season in these areas, because it’s too hot and it’s too dry.” Rain is important, as mosquitoes need standing water to breed: This is where they lay their eggs, and where the larvae and pupae develop. “If it rains too little, then there’s not sufficient breeding sites,” explains Felipe Colón González, technology lead and expert in climate and infectious diseases at the UK-based health foundation Wellcome. “But if it rains too much, then it can wash away the mosquito breeding sites.” Humidity also plays a role. Beyond mosquitoes, climate conditions can have different effects on the viruses they carry, and on the behavior of humans—an important consideration in the spread of disease. This all makes modeling patterns of mosquitoes and the diseases they spread more complex, so ongoing surveillance is critical. It may be possible to reduce mosquito populations if they’re caught early enough. ENEA’s Moretti is part of a team that recently ran an experiment to control a tiger mosquito population in Italy by releasing more than a million male mosquitoes that were reproductively incompatible—meaning when they mate, the female lays sterile eggs. This significantly reduced the number of fertile mosquito eggs in the area. But breeding mosquitoes in a specialized biofactory was expensive. In this case, the mosquitoes were bred in Miami by Google’s Debug program and shipped to Italy. In any case, it would be impossible to eliminate mosquitoes, Moretti says—they’re too efficient at colonizing new areas. “But what is important is to drastically reduce the risks of transmissions of tropical viruses.” Colón González says the main test for European countries is whether public health systems can keep isolated outbreaks from becoming major crises. “A common misconception is that preparedness is mainly about predicting disease outbreaks,” he says. “In reality, preparedness is about building the system that allows the public health infrastructure to effectively react when the risks emerge.” In this regard, Europe has an advantage over some parts of the world: Reliable public health and information systems mean that, so far, cases of mosquito-transmitted diseases have remained lower than in other areas of the world with a similar climate. As invasive mosquitoes continue to settle across the Continent, Europeans may have to get used to taking the kinds of precautions that people in tropical regions are accustomed to. In Spain, Bartumeus already has mosquito nets on his windows and regularly empties standing water, such as that left sitting in flower pots. He uses mosquito repellant and wears long trousers if he’s going to be outside in the evening. “Simple things like that, that’s the only thing you can do,” he says. Soon, the tiger mosquito may have company. Another invasive species, Aedes aegypti, or the yellow fever mosquito, has already established populations in Cyprus and the Portuguese archipelago of Madeira. As its name would suggest, it can transmit yellow fever, but it also carries dengue, chikungunya, and Zika. Yellow fever mosquitoes can’t tolerate temperatures quite as cool as the tiger mosquito can, but it has been detected making occasional jaunts north, likely transported on cargo. Its most recent destination: a service station in Luxembourg
View five news elements
Time
Publication date: 2026-07-29. Event period: Mid-August to mid-October 2024 for the Fano outbreak.
Location and population
Country: Italy (Fano). Affected population: Patients with dengue symptoms in a specific neighborhood of Fano. Broader context: Europe, with invasive mosquitoes spreading across the continent.
Event
Confirmed local outbreak of dengue virus in Fano, Italy, in 2024. The outbreak involved 199 cases and was notable as the largest local dengue outbreak in mainland Europe at the time. Patients had not traveled to endemic regions, indicating local transmission.
Scale, impact and risk
Cases: 199 detected in Fano between mid-August and mid-October 2024. Spread: Local transmission within a neighborhood in Fano. Risk: Ongoing risk of tropical diseases becoming endemic in Europe due to invasive mosquitoes (Aedes albopictus) and climate change. Other mosquito-borne viruses like chikungunya and West Nile virus have also been reported in Europe.
Response, status and uncertainty
Investigation: Confirmed dengue cases through testing. Response: Experimentation with mosquito population control (e.g., sterile male mosquitoes) in Italy. Uncertainty: Future risk of endemic transmission depends on climate conditions, mosquito adaptation, and public health system preparedness. Source limitation: Full original article was reviewed (content_status: full).

为什么糖尿病会使登革热变得更加危险?

Why Does Diabetes Make Dengue More Dangerous?
新闻简报糖尿病和传染病专家最近的一项审查强调,2 型糖尿病患者可能面临更高的 重症登革热 并发症风险,包括登革出血热和休克综合征。该综述表明,持续升高的血糖会损害免疫功能、损害血管并促进过度炎症,可能导致登革热感染期间出现更严重的后果。基于这些发现,作者建议在登革热流行地区考虑将登革热 疫苗接种 作为综合糖尿病护理的一部分。然而,他们强调,疫苗接种 政策应遵循国家指导方针和个人资格标准。该评论还强调了预防蚊虫叮咬、保持良好的血糖控制以及糖尿病患者在登革热季节发烧时尽早就医的重要性。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Why Does Diabetes Make Dengue More Dangerous?

News Brief
A recent review by diabetes and infectious disease experts highlights that individuals with type 2 diabetes may face a higher risk of severe dengue complications, including dengue hemorrhagic fever and shock syndrome. The review suggests that persistently elevated blood sugar can impair immune function, damage blood vessels, and promote excessive inflammation, potentially leading to more severe outcomes during dengue infection. Based on these findings, the authors recommend considering dengue vaccination as part of comprehensive diabetes care in regions where dengue is endemic. However, they emphasize that vaccination policies should follow national guidelines and individual eligibility criteria. The review also underscores the importance of mosquito bite prevention, maintaining good blood sugar control, and seeking early medical care if fever develops during dengue season for people with diabetes.

Why Does Diabetes Make Dengue More Dangerous? Written by Dr. Nikitha Narayanan Medically Reviewed by Dr. Shanmathi Rajendran BDS on Jul 24 2026 2:43 PM Listen to this article 0:00/0:00 People with type 2 diabetes may face a higher risk of severe dengue complications, including dengue hemorrhagic fever and shock syndrome. Learn how diabetes influences dengue severity and why experts recommend integrating infectious disease prevention into diabetes care. Highlights: A review suggests people with type 2 diabetes are more likely to develop severe dengue complications High blood sugar may impair immunity and increase the risk of excessive inflammation during dengue infection Experts recommend considering dengue vaccination for eligible people with diabetes in dengue-endemic regions Dengue fever affects millions of people every year, but not everyone develops severe disease (1✔ ✔Trusted SourceDiabetes as risk factor to severity of dengue in naive patientsGo to source). According to a new review by diabetes and infectious disease experts, people living with type 2 diabetes are more likely to experience serious complications, including dengue hemorrhagic fever and dengue shock syndrome. The authors suggest that diabetes should be recognized as an important risk factor when planning dengue prevention strategies. Blood Sugar ChartThe blood sugar chart gives you the fasting glucose values and glucose tolerance test values for normal people and people with early diabetes and established diabetes. Also use the calculator to find out if you have diabetes. How Can High Blood Sugar Worsen Dengue Infection? Researchers explain that persistently elevated blood sugar can affect several parts of the immune system. Poorly controlled diabetes may: Reduce the body's ability to fight infections Damage blood vessels Promote chronic inflammation Increase the likelihood of an exaggerated immune response, sometimes called a cytokine storm During dengue infection, this excessive inflammatory response may contribute to: Dengue hemorrhagic fever Internal bleeding Plasma leakage Dengue shock syndrome Diabetes Risk Assessment CalculatorAlmost one-third of the people are unaware of the risk factors of diabetes. Find out if you run the risk of diabetes by using Diabetes Risk Assessment calculator.Why Diabetes May Increase Severe Dengue Risk Diabetes-Related Change Possible Effect During Dengue High blood sugar Impaired immune response. Blood vessel damage Greater risk of vascular leakage. Chronic inflammation Stronger inflammatory response. Cytokine storm Increased risk of severe dengue complications. Dengue Vaccine – Administer Only in High Burden RegionsNewly developed dengue vaccine should be used only if you are previously infected by the dengue virus. A blood test before taking the vaccine is recommended.Should Dengue Vaccination Become Part of Diabetes Care? One of the most important recommendations from the review is to consider integrating dengue vaccination into routine diabetes management, particularly in regions where dengue is common. The authors argue that preventing infection in high-risk individuals could reduce: Severe illness Hospitalization Complications Healthcare burden during outbreaks However, they do not recommend universal vaccination for everyone with diabetes. Vaccination policies should continue to follow national guidelines, vaccine eligibility criteria, and individual clinical assessment. DengiAll: Why India's First Dengue Vaccine is Being Watched WorldwideDengiAll emerges as a promising single-dose solution offering balanced protection against all four dengue types.Why Managing Chronic Diseases and Infectious Diseases Together Makes Sense Traditionally, diabetes care has focused on controlling blood sugar and preventing complications such as heart disease, kidney disease, and nerve damage. The review proposes a broader approach by integrating infectious disease prevention into routine diabetes care. This could include: Vaccination where appropriate Patient education during dengue season Early recognition of warning signs Prompt medical evaluation during fever Such an approach may improve outcomes in countries where dengue is endemic. Which Symptoms Need Immediate Medical Attention? People with diabetes who develop dengue should seek urgent medical care if they experience: Persistent vomiting Severe abdominal pain Bleeding from the gums or nose Blood in vomit or stools Extreme weakness Difficulty breathing Dizziness or fainting Reduced urine output These may indicate progression to severe dengue. What This Means for You People living with type 2 diabetes already face a higher risk of several long-term health complications. This review suggests they may also be more vulnerable to severe dengue, reinforcing the importance of preventing mosquito bites, maintaining good blood sugar control, and seeking early medical care if fever develops during dengue season. The authors also propose that dengue vaccination should be considered as part of comprehensive diabetes care in endemic regions. However, because this is a review paper rather than a new clinical trial, and because dengue vaccines have specific eligibility criteria, further research and public health guidance will be needed before such an approach becomes routine. Frequently Asked QuestionsQ: Does diabetes increase the risk of severe dengue?A: Evidence summarized in this review suggests that people with type 2 diabetes are more likely to develop severe dengue complications than those without diabetes.Q: Why does diabetes make dengue more severe?A: High blood sugar can impair immune function, damage blood vessels, and promote excessive inflammation, which may contribute to severe dengue.Q: Should everyone with diabetes get a dengue vaccine?A: Not necessarily. Dengue vaccination should follow national guidelines and individual eligibility criteria because current vaccines are not recommended for everyone.Q: Can good blood sugar control reduce dengue complications?A: Although this review did not directly study blood sugar control, maintaining good glucose control supports overall immune health and is generally recommended during infections.Q: What should people with diabetes do during dengue season?A: Prevent mosquito bites, control blood sugar, stay hydrated, and seek prompt medical attention if fever or dengue warning signs develop.
