登革热 美国病例现已突破 500 例,传播的蚊子正在向北扩张 - inkl

Dengue Fever Has Now Surpassed 500 U.S. Cases and the Mosquito That Spreads It Is Expanding North - inkl
新闻简报2026 年,美国的 登革热 病例已超过 500 例,与 2024 年报告的破纪录的 3,798 例相比,继续呈显着上升趋势。主要蚊子 媒介 埃及伊蚊正在向北扩大其活动范围,使当地 传播 出现在以前被认为是低风险的地区,包括洛杉矶县,该县在 2025 年记录了持续的社区传播。佛罗里达州仍然是风险最高的州,迈阿密戴德县占当地病例的大多数,但德克萨斯州南部和加利福尼亚州的主要都市区也面临较高的风险。公共卫生当局对不断扩大的风险感到担忧,尤其是随着旺季(七月至九月)的持续。美国大陆没有广泛使用的 疫苗,响应工作集中在 媒介 控制上。波多黎各仍然受到严重影响,已进入公共卫生紧急状态。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026 年 7 月 24 日。事件期间:2026 年,7 月至 9 月为病例高峰。
地点与对象
美国,特别是佛罗里达州(迈阿密戴德县和其他县)、加利福尼亚州(洛杉矶都市区)、德克萨斯州南部以及夏洛特、亚特兰大、菲尼克斯、洛杉矶和圣地亚哥等主要都市区。受影响人群:美国居民(佛罗里达州南部风险最高)以及前往加勒比海、墨西哥和中美洲的旅行者。
事件
2026年,美国确诊的登革热病例已超过500例,较2024年(3,798例)继续呈上升趋势。蚊子 媒介 埃及伊蚊正在向北扩大其活动范围,使当地的 传播 能够进入新的地区。2025 年,洛杉矶县记录了在当地获得的 传播 的为期七周的连锁反应。
规模、影响与风险
病例:2026 年将有 500 多例(据报道),2024 年将有 3,798 例(CDC ArboNET)。死亡人数:波多黎各 13 人(2024 年)。住院治疗:波多黎各 6,291 例病例中的 52%(2024 年)。风险:佛罗里达州最高,加利福尼亚州、德克萨斯州也出现局部 传播,以及由于蚊子范围扩大而出现的更北的风险区域。
应对、状态与不确定性
回应:媒介 控制被强调为主要干预措施。美国大陆没有批准登革热 疫苗 通用;Dengvaxia 即将停产。不确定性:本地 暴发 将以多快的速度和广泛的范围扩展到佛罗里达州南部以外;全球登革热活动对进口事件的影响。来源限制:已审查完整的原始文章(content_status:完整)。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue Fever Has Now Surpassed 500 U.S. Cases and the Mosquito That Spreads It Is Expanding North - inkl

News Brief
Dengue fever cases in the United States have exceeded 500 in 2026, continuing a significant upward trend from the record-breaking 3,798 cases reported in 2024. The primary mosquito vector, Aedes aegypti, is expanding its range northward, enabling local transmission in areas previously considered low-risk, including Los Angeles County, where a sustained community spread was documented in 2025. Florida remains the highest-risk state, with Miami-Dade County accounting for most local cases, but southern Texas and California's major metro areas also face elevated risk. Public health authorities are concerned about the expanding risk, particularly as the peak season (July-September) continues. No vaccine is widely available for the continental U.S., and response efforts focus on vector control. Puerto Rico remains severely affected, with a public health emergency in effect.
Original source text (English)
Get all your news in one place. 100's of premium titles. One app. Start reading Get all your news in one place. 100's of premium titles. One news app. Start reading Medical Daily Cole Mercer Jul 24, 2026, 12:00 PM Dengue Fever Has Now Surpassed 500 U.S. Cases and the Mosquito That Spreads It Is Expanding North Florida CDC Los Angeles United States MMWR Public Health Dengue fever has surpassed 500 cases in the United States in 2026, following a record-breaking 2024 that saw 3,798 confirmed cases reported to the CDC's ArboNET surveillance system , a 359 percent increase above the 2010 to 2023 annual average. The mosquito primarily responsible for spreading the virus, Aedes aegypti, is now confirmed to be expanding its range northward into states well beyond its traditional subtropical stronghold. And this summer, the window for local transmission risk extends further into the continental United States than it did a decade ago. Public health authorities, entomologists, and infectious disease specialists are tracking the shift with concern. Dengue is not simply a travel disease any longer for Americans. While the vast majority of cases remain travel-associated, the conditions enabling local transmission are broadening with each passing season. Why This Matters Most people who contract dengue experience a flu-like illness and recover fully. But dengue is unusual among mosquito-borne diseases in that a second infection with a different dengue serotype, there are four, can trigger severe hemorrhagic fever. This condition causes the blood vessels to leak, platelets to drop, and in serious cases can lead to organ failure and death. There is currently no approved dengue vaccine recommended for general use in the continental United States, and no specific antiviral treatment. The cases most visible to U.S. readers are concentrated in Florida, where Miami-Dade County has maintained an active mosquito-borne illness alert through 2026. But the more significant public health story is what is happening further north. A peer-reviewed paper in the CDC's Emerging Infectious Diseases journal documented a seven-week chain of locally acquired dengue transmission in Los Angeles County in 2025, the kind of sustained community spread once considered impossible outside the subtropical south. What We Know So Far The 2024 MMWR report on dengue in the United States is the most comprehensive recent picture of the national burden. Of the 3,798 cases reported that year, 97.2 percent were travel-associated, with most acquired in the Caribbean, Central America, or Mexico. But 2.8 percent, approximately 106 cases, were locally acquired, a number that, while small in absolute terms, was significantly higher than in most prior years. The seasonal pattern is consistent and important: dengue cases peak between July and September, accounting for 41.6 percent of annual cases, according to the same MMWR. That means the current period, mid-summer 2026, is the highest-risk window of the year. Cases in 2026 have already passed 500 as of the brief period so far this year, continuing the upward trend that marked 2024. Florida reported 1,044 local and travel-associated dengue cases in 2024, more than any other continental state. California followed with 719 cases, driven largely by travel-associated infections in the Los Angeles metro area, though the documented local transmission chain in Los Angeles County has raised the profile of the state as an emerging risk area. Where the Risk Is Highest Florida remains the highest-risk state for locally acquired dengue in the continental United States. Miami-Dade County's subtropical climate sustains Aedes aegypti populations year-round, and the county accounts for the large majority of Florida's locally transmitted cases. Hillsborough, Pasco, Manatee, Monroe, Orange, Palm Beach, Broward, Sarasota, and Polk counties in Florida all reported locally acquired cases in 2024. Southern Texas and California's major metro areas present the next tier of risk. The more notable development is the northward range extension of Aedes aegypti and its close relative Aedes albopictus, commonly called the tiger mosquito. Published research from the University at Albany , released in September 2024, attributed dengue risk expansion in California, Arizona, and the Carolinas to the documented range extension of both species. A 2026 mosquito surveillance report projects continued expansion into previously unaffected northern states, with localized dengue transmission risk expected to grow in new geographies. In practical terms, residents of Charlotte, Atlanta, Phoenix, Los Angeles, and San Diego now live within the potential range of mosquitoes capable of transmitting dengue. That does not mean dengue cases will inevitably occur in those cities, but the biological conditions are now present in a way they were not a generation ago. What Doctors and Experts Say The University of Florida's Emerging Pathogens Institute has noted that while no part of the continental United States is officially classified as endemic for dengue, Florida's health department continues to report locally acquired cases each year, and the risk is no longer limited to its southernmost counties. Researchers at the institute have linked the resurgence of Aedes aegypti in Florida, after a period of near-displacement by Aedes albopictus in the 1990s, to the conditions that have enabled more frequent local transmission since 2009. The CDC's public health guidance for jurisdictions facing dengue transmission risk emphasizes vector control as the primary intervention when Aedes mosquito populations are high, temperatures and precipitation support mosquito activity, and travel-associated case counts are elevated. All three conditions now apply in multiple U.S. metropolitan areas during the summer months. Puerto Rico remains the most severely affected U.S. territory, having reported 6,291 dengue cases in 2024, with more than 52 percent of those requiring hospitalization and 13 deaths. A public health emergency remains in effect there. What the Evidence Shows and What It Does Not The evidence that dengue risk is expanding in the continental United States is supported by multiple lines of data: CDC ArboNET surveillance showing rising case counts, peer-reviewed entomological research documenting vector range expansion, a confirmed local transmission chain in Los Angeles, and sustained activity in Florida. What remains uncertain is how quickly and how broadly that risk will translate into large-scale local outbreaks outside of southern Florida. The 2024 case spike was partly driven by historically high global dengue activity, which increases the probability that returning travelers will seed the virus into areas with competent mosquito populations. Whether 2026 continues that trend at the global level will affect how many importation events occur in U.S. cities this summer. The United States does not have a dengue vaccine currently recommended for general continental use. Dengvaxia , approved only for individuals aged 9 to 16 with prior confirmed dengue infection in U.S. territories including Puerto Rico, is being discontinued, with remaining stock expiring in 2026. No other dengue vaccine has received U.S. FDA approval or CDC recommendation for continental travelers or residents. Who Faces the Greatest Risk People at highest risk include residents of southern Florida, especially Miami-Dade County, and individuals who travel to Caribbean nations, Mexico, and Central America, where dengue activity remains common during summer and fall. Travelers to Puerto Rico face an ongoing elevated risk given the active outbreak status there. Anyone who has already been infected with dengue once, regardless of which serotype, faces a more serious medical risk from a subsequent infection with a different serotype. Older adults, pregnant individuals, and people with underlying health conditions such as diabetes or chronic kidney disease are most likely to experience complications from either primary or secondary infection. Residents of cities in the Carolinas, Southern California, Arizona, and the Gulf Coast region should be aware that Aedes mosquitoes capable of transmitting dengue are now present in their communities, even if locally acquired transmission remains uncommon. Symptoms and Warning Signs to Watch For Dengue symptoms typically appear four to 10 days after a mosquito bite from an infected Aedes mosquito. The classic presentation is a sudden high fever accompanied by severe headache, pain behind the eyes, muscle and joint pain, nausea, vomiting, swollen glands, and a skin rash. The joint and muscle pain can be so severe that dengue is sometimes called breakbone fever. Most people recover within one to two weeks. Severe dengue, more likely in people experiencing a second infection, can cause bleeding under the skin, gum or nose bleeds, blood in urine or stool, persistent vomiting, abdominal pain, rapid breathing, fatigue, and restlessness. Severe dengue is a medical emergency requiring immediate hospitalization. Anyone with these symptoms who has recently been in a dengue-risk area should seek emergency care immediately. What You Can Do Now Use EPA-registered mosquito repellents containing DEET, picaridin, IR3535, oil of lemon eucalyptus, or para-menthane-diol on exposed skin whenever outdoors, particularly during peak mosquito hours from dawn to dusk. Wear long sleeves and pants in areas where Aedes mosquitoes are active. Remove standing water from flowerpots, buckets, bird baths, gutters, and any container that can hold water for more than a week, as Aedes mosquitoes breed in very small amounts of still water. Travelers planning visits to dengue-risk areas should consult the CDC's destination-specific dengue guidance before departure and report any fever or flu-like illness that develops within two weeks of returning home to a health care provider, with disclosure of travel history. Anyone experiencing a high fever with severe headache and joint pain within two weeks of returning from a dengue-risk area should seek medical evaluation promptly, as early diagnosis and supportive care improve outcomes. Cost and Access: What Patients Should Know There is no specific antiviral treatment for dengue. Management is supportive, meaning rest, fluids, and pain relief with acetaminophen rather than aspirin or ibuprofen, which can worsen bleeding risk. Severe dengue requiring hospitalization is covered under most major health insurance plans. Uninsured patients who need hospital care for severe dengue should contact their county health department about emergency Medicaid enrollment or charity care programs. Dengue testing through a blood panel is available through most major diagnostic laboratories and hospital systems in areas where the disease is recognized. Clinicians in regions without prior dengue experience may need to specifically request arboviral testing panels. What Happens Next The CDC continues to update ArboNET dengue case data throughout the summer. Health departments in Florida, Texas, California, and other at-risk states are maintaining vector surveillance and issuing localized alerts as conditions warrant. Researchers tracking the Aedes aegypti range expansion are expected to publish updated findings before the end of the 2026 season. MedicalDaily will continue to track dengue activity in the United States through the fall. The Bottom Line Dengue is no longer a disease that only travelers returning from the tropics need to worry about. The record 2024 U.S. case count, the documented local transmission in Los Angeles, the sustained risk in Miami-Dade, and the confirmed northward expansion of the Aedes mosquito mean that more Americans than ever are living in areas where local dengue transmission is biologically possible during summer months. Strong mosquito repellent use, standing water elimination, and prompt medical evaluation for fever after potential exposure are the most effective protective steps available right now. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Cebu logs 381 dengue cases, 2 deaths - SunStar Publishing Inc.

News Brief
Cebu Province has reported 381 confirmed dengue cases and two deaths over the past four weeks, with health officials emphasizing that this does not constitute an outbreak but is a growing concern. Provincial Health Consultant Elisse Nicole Catalan assured residents that the rise in infections is consistent with the rainy season when mosquito breeding intensifies. Toledo City recorded the highest number of cases (49), followed by Barili (39) and Talisay City (34). The two deaths occurred in Compostela and Consolacion. All 16 province-run hospitals are prepared to handle dengue patients, with sufficient bed capacity and dedicated fast lanes activated. Diagnostic services, including complete blood count and NS1 antigen testing, remain available. However, maintaining an adequate blood supply continues to be a challenge, prompting the provincial government to organize blood donation drives on August 4 and 5.
Original source text (English)
CebuCebu logs 381 dengue cases, 2 deaths NOT an outbreak but a growing concern. Provincial health officials gave this assurance after Cebu Province recorded 381 confirmed dengue cases and two deaths over the past four weeks, urging residents to seek immediate medical attention at the first sign of symptoms. Consolidated data from the Provincial Health Office (PHO) showed Toledo City recorded the highest number of cases with 49, followed by Barili (39), Talisay City (34), Consolacion (33), Carcar City and Medellin (19 each), Cordova and Dalaguete (17 each), Sogod (14), Danao City (13), Argao, Balamban and Compostela (12 each), and Liloan (10). Other municipalities reported fewer than 10 cases each. The two reported deaths were recorded in Compostela and Consolacion. Provincial Health Consultant Elisse Nicole Catalan said on Wednesday, July 29, 2026, that the PHO continues to closely monitor all local government units with confirmed dengue cases. Despite the increase, Catalan said the province has not reached outbreak levels, noting that the rise in infections is consistent with the rainy season when mosquito breeding typically intensifies. “Epidemiologically, dili siya outbreak (It’s not an outbreak),” Catalan said. She stressed that the PHO’s priority is to encourage prompt consultation rather than cause alarm. “Amoa kay we’re really not there to cause panic or fear, but ang amo lang jud nga ibalik-balik: early detection, early treatment saves lives,” she said. (We’re not here to cause panic or fear. We simply want to keep reminding everyone that early detection and early treatment save lives) Provincial Health Officer Dr. Christina Giango Canoy said all 16 province-run hospitals remain fully prepared to handle dengue patients, with sufficient bed capacity and dedicated fast lanes already activated for individuals presenting with dengue symptoms. She said patients with mild illness are generally managed through outpatient care, while those showing warning signs or severe symptoms are admitted immediately. Diagnostic services, including complete blood count (CBC) and NS1 antigen testing, remain available in provincial hospitals to allow doctors to quickly determine whether hospitalization is necessary. Catalan emphasized that not every dengue patient requires admission. “Dili tanan dengue case kinahanglan gyud i-admit dayon sa hospital (Not every dengue case needs immediate hospitalization),” she said, explaining that early-stage and mild infections can be safely managed through close outpatient monitoring. She said rural health units and outpatient departments have also been instructed to speed up laboratory processing to ensure patients who require admission receive immediate treatment. While hospital capacity remains stable, Canoy acknowledged that maintaining an adequate blood supply continues to be a challenge. “Nihit jud ang supply sa dugo, mao nang dako kaayo og tabang kon magpa-early consultation para dili na moabot sa point nga kinahanglan na og transfusion,” she said. (Blood supply remains limited, so seeking medical attention early greatly reduces the chances of patients reaching the stage where they need blood transfusions.) Catalan likewise underscored the importance of voluntary blood donation, saying sufficient blood reserves are critical for patients who develop severe dengue and require transfusions. To help strengthen the province’s blood supply, the Cebu Provincial Government will hold blood donation drives on Aug. 4 and 5 as part of its founding anniversary activities. / CAV

癫痫患者伴有多发性颅神经病变和多系统受累的登革热相关脑病:采用纵向多模态成像的综合 病例报告 - Cureus

Dengue-Associated Encephalopathy With Multiple Cranial Neuropathies and Multisystem Involvement in a Patient With Epilepsy: A Comprehensive Case Report With Longitudinal Multimodality Imaging - Cureus
新闻简报一名 45 岁的黎巴嫩女性癫痫患者出现发烧和排尿困难,初步诊断为尿路感染。她后来出现了发作后状态,伴有严重的神经功能缺损,包括脑病、颅神经病和多系统受累。尽管脑部 MRI 正常,但 IgM 阳性仍证实了登革热感染。患者出现血小板减少、凝血障碍和心肌炎,需要 ICU 护理。在 治疗 的支持下,她的情况有所改善,并神经系统完好地出院。该病例强调了登革热即使在影像学表现不明显的情况下也有可能引起严重的神经系统表现,强调了在流行环境中早期识别的必要性。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue-Associated Encephalopathy With Multiple Cranial Neuropathies and Multisystem Involvement in a Patient With Epilepsy: A Comprehensive Case Report With Longitudinal Multimodality Imaging - Cureus

News Brief
A 45-year-old Lebanese woman with epilepsy presented with fever and dysuria, initially diagnosed with a urinary tract infection. She later developed a post-ictal state with severe neurological deficits, including encephalopathy, cranial neuropathies, and multisystem involvement. Despite normal brain MRI, dengue infection was confirmed via positive IgM. The patient experienced thrombocytopenia, coagulopathy, and myocarditis, requiring ICU care. She improved with supportive treatment and was discharged neurologically intact. This case highlights dengue's potential to cause severe neurological manifestations even with unremarkable imaging, emphasizing the need for early recognition in endemic settings.
Original source text (English)
Abstract Dengue fever is a common arboviral infection in tropical and subtropical regions. Neurological involvement is uncommon but increasingly recognized, and may mimic stroke, post-ictal deficits, thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura, or sepsis-associated encephalopathy. We report a 45-year-old Lebanese woman with epilepsy who presented after a short febrile illness with dysuria initially treated as a urinary tract infection. She was later found in a post-ictal state with frothy oral secretions and urinary incontinence, febrile to 40°C and hypotensive to 70/50 mmHg. Neurological examination revealed reduced consciousness, abnormal rightward gaze, right-sided neck dystonia, nystagmus, left lower motor neuron facial palsy, suspected left sixth nerve palsy, and left-sided hemiparesis. Brain MRI and MR venography showed no acute infarction or venous sinus thrombosis. During admission, her platelet count fell from 200 × 109/L to 23 × 109/L, with an international normalized ratio of 2.6 and elevated D-dimer. Creatine kinase, troponin, and creatine kinase-myocardial band (CK-MB) were markedly raised, and echocardiography showed an ejection fraction of 35-40%, consistent with myocarditis. Dengue IgM was positive on Day 5 of illness. She was treated in the intensive care unit with hemodynamic support, empiric antibiotics, and pulse corticosteroids. Her neurological and laboratory findings improved gradually over a 14-day admission, including seven days in intensive care, and she was discharged home neurologically intact (modified Rankin Scale 1). Dengue should be considered in patients with acute febrile illness and unexplained neurological deficits, particularly in endemic settings. Early recognition is important because dengue can present with encephalopathy, cranial neuropathies, and multisystem dysfunction despite unremarkable neuroimaging. Introduction Dengue fever is an arboviral disease caused by dengue virus, a Flavivirus transmitted to humans by Aedes mosquitoes, mainly Aedes aegypti. Dengue virus has four serotypes (DEN-1 to DEN-4), with DEN-2 and DEN-3 more often associated with severe forms such as dengue hemorrhagic fever and dengue shock syndrome. The clinical spectrum ranges from asymptomatic infection and non-specific viral illness to life-threatening shock and multiorgan failure [1-5]. This report follows the Clinical Case Reports emphasis on a clear clinical message, practical learning point, patient consent, and generalisable best-practice relevance [6-9]. Dengue virus infection can trigger strong immune responses, and in secondary infections this may worsen the illness by causing plasma leakage, shock, and organ involvement [1-4]. Although dengue virus was historically considered non-neurotropic, accumulating evidence from clinical series and case reports suggests that it can cross the blood-brain barrier or affect the nervous system indirectly through systemic inflammation, metabolic disturbances, and immune-mediated mechanisms, broadening the concept of neurological dengue [10-15]. In practical terms, direct neurotropism refers to the virus itself invading nervous tissue, whereas indirect neurologic effects occur when the body's own immune and inflammatory response to systemic dengue infection damages the nervous system without the virus directly attacking it. Neurological manifestations of dengue are relatively rare but increasingly reported, including encephalopathy, encephalitis, acute symptomatic seizures, Guillain-Barré syndrome, myelitis, myositis, and various cranial mononeuropathies. Because such presentations often include fever, altered mental status, focal deficits, thrombocytopenia, and coagulopathy, they may be misdiagnosed as stroke, bacterial meningoencephalitis, thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura (TTP), disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC), or autoimmune disease. We describe a middle-aged woman with epilepsy who developed dengue-associated encephalopathy with cranial neuropathies and multisystem involvement, an unusual combination that, together with entirely normal neuroimaging, highlights the diagnostic challenges and the importance of maintaining a high index of suspicion for dengue in endemic settings [10-15]. Case reports from endemic regions are essential to disseminate best practice, clarify diagnostic workup, and illustrate how international dengue guidelines are applied in real-world neurological presentations [1-5,10-16]. Case Presentation A 45-year-old divorced Lebanese woman, known to have adult-onset epilepsy for approximately seven months (diagnosed in her mid-40s), presented with fever and dysuria one to two days before admission. She was evaluated in the emergency department (ED), diagnosed with a urinary tract infection on the basis of both clinical symptoms and objective urinalysis findings (positive nitrites and abundant pus cells (>50 WBC/high-power field (HPF); reference <5 WBC/HPF)). Urine culture was sent but results were not available at the time of the initial ED visit; the diagnosis was supported by the clinical and laboratory picture, and she was discharged on oral antibiotics and paracetamol. She had no other significant comorbidities. Her epilepsy had been managed with levetiracetam 500 mg twice daily; however, she had recently self-reduced the dose to once daily because the morning dose caused daytime somnolence that interfered with her job as a makeup artist. She was a smoker and lived with her mother and three brothers, who provided social and financial support. On the morning of admission, her mother found her unresponsive in bed, with frothy oral secretions and urinary incontinence, suggestive of a prolonged generalized tonic-clonic seizure. Emergency medical services transported her to the ED. On arrival, she was febrile with a temperature of 40°C and hypotensive with a blood pressure of 70/50 mmHg. Her heart rate and oxygen saturation on room air were within normal limits. Clinically she was deeply unconscious and minimally responsive even to deep painful stimuli, appearing profoundly ill. Aggressive intravenous fluid resuscitation with normal saline was initiated, after which her blood pressure began to improve. A provisional diagnosis of post-ictal state due to breakthrough seizure was initially considered, supported by her history of epilepsy with recent medication non-adherence and the classic post-ictal findings on presentation; however, this diagnosis was rapidly challenged by features atypical for a simple post-ictal state - a high fever of 40°C (post-ictal fever is uncommon and usually low-grade), hypotension of 70/50 mmHg, and multiple focal neurological deficits that a post-ictal state alone would not explain - prompting a broader differential diagnosis. She received benzodiazepines and broad-spectrum antibiotics in view of possible sepsis. Initial neurological examination revealed rightward deviation of gaze, right-sided neck dystonia, and horizontal nystagmus to the right. There was ophthalmoplegia with a suspected left sixth cranial nerve palsy, left lower motor neuron facial weakness consistent with Bell's palsy, and left-sided hemiparesis. Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) was estimated at E1V1M3-4 (approximately 5-6/15), consistent with severe impairment of consciousness. Pupils were equal and reactive to light bilaterally, with no anisocoria and no papilledema on fundoscopy, arguing against uncal herniation and suggesting preserved brainstem function. No neck stiffness or Kernig/Brudzinski signs were elicited, an important negative finding against bacterial meningitis, although meningismus can be absent in deeply comatose patients; a Babinski sign was not explicitly documented given the patient's depressed consciousness. Given the acute focal deficits, the working differential included ischemic stroke, venous sinus thrombosis, meningoencephalitis, dengue-associated encephalopathy, thrombotic microangiopathy, and sepsis-associated coagulopathy. Timeline of clinical course The clinical course is summarized in Table 1. Investigations and differential diagnosis A comprehensive multimodality investigative workup was conducted. Serial laboratory results with units and reference ranges are presented in Table 2. Initial blood counts showed normal hemoglobin and total white cell count with a platelet count of approximately 200 × 109/L. Urinalysis demonstrated positive nitrites and abundant pus cells. Liver function tests revealed mildly elevated transaminases. Chest radiography on day one was unremarkable (Figure 1). Brain MRI with gadolinium contrast (Day 2) employed multiplanar T1, T2, fluid-attenuated inversion recovery (FLAIR), paired diffusion-weighted imaging (DWI) and apparent diffusion coefficient (ADC) sequences (reviewed in tandem; no areas of restricted diffusion or corresponding low ADC signal were identified, effectively excluding acute ischemic stroke within the imaging window and helping distinguish cytotoxic from vasogenic edema), susceptibility-weighted imaging (SWI), and post-contrast T1 fat-saturation sequences (Figure 2). No acute infarction, hemorrhage, collection, or abnormal parenchymal enhancement was identified. Importantly, the study revealed expanded extra-axial cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) spaces at the frontoparietal regions, expanded superior cerebellar cistern, partial empty sella, bilateral distended perioptic nerve sheaths, and a right frontal retention cyst, with a normal ventricular system and no midline shift. These findings - expanded extra-axial CSF spaces, partial empty sella, and perioptic nerve sheath distension - are recognized imaging markers that may suggest alteration of CSF pressure dynamics. Their stability on comparison with a prior MRI obtained one year earlier, and their confirmation on the two-month follow-up scan, support their interpretation as a pre-existing chronic condition rather than an acute dengue-induced change, although formal CSF pressure measurement was not performed. A small right parietal intra-osseous lesion consistent with a benign haemangioma and mild right maxillary sinusitis were incidental findings of no clinical significance and are not discussed further. No abnormal meningeal enhancement was seen. Magnetic resonance angiography (MRA) of the intracranial vessels (Day 2) demonstrated patent anterior and posterior circulation with no arterial occlusion, stenosis, aneurysm, or arteriovenous malformation, effectively excluding acute ischemic stroke (Figure 3). Magnetic resonance venography (MRV) of the brain (Day 2) showed patent dural venous sinuses with no evidence of thrombosis. The right transverse sinus was attenuated/hypoplastic, a recognized anatomical variant. These findings effectively excluded cerebral venous sinus thrombosis (Figures 4). Abdominal and pelvic ultrasound (Day 2) demonstrated mild hepatosplenomegaly (spleen 15.5 cm), engorged inferior vena cava (IVC) and intrahepatic veins, and minimal to mild free intraabdominal fluid, consistent with systemic dengue-related venous congestion and plasma leakage; a right renal complex cystic lesion, a right simple ovarian cyst, and small thyroid findings were incidental and are not discussed further (Figure 5). Non-contrast CT of the abdomen and pelvis was requested after the ultrasound to characterize the extent of plasma leakage, to exclude bowel ischemia, intestinal obstruction, or another acute abdominal process in a hypotensive patient, and to assess for hydronephrosis or obstructive uropathy given the rising creatinine. CT (Day 2) confirmed mild ascites and diffuse subcutaneous edematous changes of the abdominal walls consistent with dengue-related plasma leakage. It showed normal bilateral kidneys without back-pressure changes or calculi, with no pneumoperitoneum or intestinal obstruction identified (Figure 6). Bilateral lower limb venous Doppler ultrasound (Day 2) demonstrated patent bilateral deep veins with no evidence of deep vein thrombosis (DVT). Bedside arterial duplex ultrasound of the left limbs (Day 3) was performed because the patient's left-sided hemiparesis, in the setting of cardiac dysfunction (left ventricular ejection fraction (LVEF) 35-40%) and coagulopathy, raised concern for peripheral arterial embolism or dengue-associated vasculopathy; it demonstrated patent left limb arteries without stenosis or occlusion and a normal arterial waveform, effectively excluding embolism or vasculitis, though it incidentally identified a 1.5 × 1.0 cm intramuscular hematoma at the left elbow consistent with dengue-related coagulopathic bleeding at a prior access site. Bedside neck ultrasonography (Day 3) showed bilateral reactive-appearing cervical lymph nodes, consistent with a systemic infectious response; both carotid arteries were patent. Bedside electroencephalography (EEG) was performed on Day 2 after initial stabilization. It showed diffuse generalized theta and delta slowing consistent with encephalopathy, with no epileptiform discharges, no electrographic seizures, and no periodic lateralized epileptiform discharges. This supported a diagnosis of encephalopathy rather than ongoing status epilepticus, and the persistence of diffuse slowing beyond 48 hours favored dengue-associated encephalopathy over a simple post-ictal pattern, which typically resolves within 24 hours. A formal prolonged video-EEG was not performed given the patient's critical condition. A follow-up MRI brain without contrast was performed two months after the acute dengue episode, in the context of the patient's known epilepsy with recurrence of seizures. Findings were stationary compared with the acute admission MRI, confirming that the expanded extra-axial CSF spaces, partial empty sella, and perioptic nerve sheath distension represent a pre-existing chronic condition rather than an acute dengue-induced change. No new parenchymal lesions, post-infectious changes, or late sequelae of dengue encephalopathy were identified. Bedside neck ultrasonography (Day 3) showed bilateral reactive-appearing cervical lymph nodes with preserved fatty hila and oval morphology, consistent with a systemic infectious response. Both thyroid glands were of average size with mild increased vascularity; a right thyroid nodule (TI-RADS 1, 1.0 × 0.7 cm - benign, no fine needle aspiration required) and a small left thyroid cyst (0.2 × 0.3 cm) were identified. Both carotid arteries were patent. A follow-up MRI brain without contrast was performed two months after the acute dengue episode in the context of the patient's known epilepsy with recurrence of seizures (Figure 7). Multiplanar T1, PD, T2, FLAIR, DWI, and SWI sequences were obtained. Findings demonstrated expanded extra-axial CSF spaces at the frontoparietal regions; expanded superior cerebellar cistern with partial empty sella; normal ventricular system with no midline shift; no abnormal cerebral parenchymal signal intensity lesions; preserved cortical sulci and grey-white matter differentiation bilaterally; no brainstem or cerebellar lesions; no mesial temporal sclerosis; and a right frontal retention cyst. The radiologist's opinion concluded expanded extra-axial CSF spaces with partial empty sella, which could suggest alteration of CSF pressure. These findings were stationary when compared with the acute admission MRI of Day 2, confirming that the expanded CSF spaces, empty sella, and perioptic nerve sheath distension represent a pre-existing chronic condition rather than an acute dengue-induced change. No new parenchymal lesions, post-infectious changes, or late sequelae of dengue encephalopathy were identified. Over the subsequent 24-48 hours from admission, the platelet count fell to a nadir of 23 × 109/L. Coagulation studies showed an international normalised ratio (INR) of 2.6 and markedly elevated D-dimer with normal fibrinogen and reticulocyte counts. Peripheral blood smear showed only a few schistocytes (<1/HPF), arguing against fulminant thrombotic microangiopathy, although ADAMTS13 testing was unavailable. Serum creatinine rose from 0.9 to 1.6 mg/dL. Creatine kinase (CK) peaked at ~7,000 U/L, troponin at 711 ng/L, and creatine kinase-myocardial band (CK-MB) at 43 U/L; myoglobinuria was absent. Echocardiography demonstrated global hypokinesia with LVEF of 35-40%, consistent with dengue myocarditis. Electrocardiogram showed no significant arrhythmia. Autoimmune workup revealed positive antinuclear antibodies (ANA; speckled, low-to-moderate titre); antiphospholipid antibodies were negative. Dengue IgM enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) was positive on Day 5, confirming acute primary dengue infection; dengue IgG was negative. Blood cultures (two sets, Day 1) and urine culture showed no growth at 48 hours (blood cultures remained negative at five days), supporting de-escalation of empiric antibiotics once the clinical course and Day-5 dengue IgM positivity pointed to a viral rather than bacterial etiology. Lumbar puncture was deferred due to severe thrombocytopenia, coagulopathy, and hemodynamic instability. ADAMTS13 testing was unavailable. Management and outcome The patient was transferred to the intensive care unit for close monitoring and organ support. She received aggressive intravenous fluid resuscitation and vasopressor support as needed during the early hypotensive phase. Empirical broad-spectrum antibiotics were administered to cover possible severe bacterial sepsis and urinary tract infection; these initially included a carbapenem-based regimen (Days 1-3) and were de-escalated to piperacillin-tazobactam on Day 4 once cultures returned negative and the clinical course was consistent with dengue, and discontinued entirely by Day 6. Given the significant neurological deficits, normal neuroimaging, and the possibility of immune-mediated encephalopathy or cranial neuropathies, pulse corticosteroid therapy was initiated in the form of high-dose intravenous dexamethasone (total 500 mg over pulse dosing). 印度批准首个登革热 疫苗:医生解释其功效、副作用以及谁应该接种 - Bhaskar English

India approves first dengue vaccine: Doctor explains its efficacy, side effects, and who should get it - Bhaskar English
新闻简报印度已批准其首款登革热疫苗,QDENGA(TAK-003),由武田制药公司开发。该疫苗适用于4至60岁人群,可提供对所有四种登革病毒类型的保护,并在18个月内将住院风险降低90%。登革热是全球传播速度最快的蚊媒疾病,印度在2025年报告了1.21万例病例和131例死亡。该疫苗已获得DCGI批准并取得WHO预认证,临床试验涉及全球28,000名参与者,其中包括印度480名。尽管有效,但它不能提供100%的保护,这强调了继续采取蚊虫预防措施的必要性。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

India approves first dengue vaccine: Doctor explains its efficacy, side effects, and who should get it - Bhaskar English

News Brief
India has approved its first dengue vaccine, QDENGA (TAK-003), developed by Takeda Pharmaceuticals. The vaccine, approved for individuals aged 4 to 60 years, provides protection against all four types of dengue virus and reduces hospitalization risk by 90% within 18 months. With dengue being the fastest-spreading mosquito-borne disease globally, India reported 1.21 lakh cases and 131 deaths in 2025. The vaccine has been approved by the DCGI and received WHO prequalification, with clinical trials involving 28,000 participants worldwide, including 480 in India. While effective, it does not offer 100% protection, emphasizing the need for continued mosquito prevention measures.
Original source text (English)
English News Lifestyle India Approves First Dengue Vaccine QDENGA | Benefits & Side Effects India approves first dengue vaccine: Doctor explains its efficacy, side effects, and who should get it 4 days ago Copy link As soon as the rain arrives, the fear of dengue also starts knocking. Every year, millions of people fall prey to this mosquito-borne disease, and many patients even lose their lives. In such a situation, India has received a vaccine for the prevention of dengue for the first time. This vaccine, named 'QDENGA' (TAK-003), has been approved by DCGI (Drug Controller General of India). This vaccine is designed to develop the body's immunity against all four types of dengue virus. Dengue is the fastest-spreading mosquito-borne disease in the world. According to the 'World Health Organization' (WHO), more than 1.46 crore cases of dengue were reported worldwide in 2024. These are the highest cases ever recorded in a single year. According to the Union Health Ministry, more than 1.21 lakh cases of dengue were registered across the country in 2025. In this, 131 people died. In such a situation, the arrival of this vaccine will help in preventing dengue. Therefore, today we will discuss the 'QDENGA' vaccine in detail. We will also know that- In which countries of the world is this vaccine already being used? How effective is the 'QDENGA' vaccine? Expert- Dr. Sanchayan Roy, Senior Consultant, Internal Medicine, Apollo Spectra Hospital, Delhi What is 'QDENGA', the dengue vaccine approved in India? It is a tetravalent vaccine that protects against dengue. It develops immunity in the body against the dengue virus and protects against all four types of dengue (DENV-1, DENV-2, DENV-3, and DENV-4). It also reduces the risk of severe dengue. Which company has developed it? Japanese pharmaceutical company 'Takeda Pharmaceuticals' has developed the QDENGA vaccine. The company developed it after many years of research and clinical trials. Who has approved it in India? In India, the QDENGA vaccine has been approved by the 'Drug Controller General of India' (DCGI). DCGI permits the use of new drugs and vaccines after checking their safety and efficacy. In which countries of the world is this vaccine already being used? Before India, QDENGA has been approved in 42 countries worldwide. These include member countries of the European Union (EU), United Kingdom (UK), Switzerland, Indonesia, Malaysia, and Thailand. See the list of major countries where this vaccine is being used in the graphic- On how many people was this vaccine trial conducted? See in detail pointers- According to the press release from 'Takeda Biopharmaceuticals India', a total of 19 clinical trials (Phase-1, Phase-2, and Phase-3) for the QDENGA vaccine have been conducted worldwide. Approximately 28,000 people were included in these trials. In India, a Phase-3 trial was conducted between March 2024 and May 2025, involving 480 participants. The objective of this trial was to assess the safety, efficacy, and immune response generated by the vaccine in the Indian population. Question - In which countries was it trialled? How many years did the trial last? Answer - Its clinical trial took place in dengue-affected countries in Asia and Latin America. These countries include - India Brazil Colombia Dominican Republic Nicaragua Panama Philippines Sri Lanka Thailand Research on the dengue vaccine has been ongoing worldwide for the past two to three decades. During this period, several Phase-1, Phase-2, and Phase-3 clinical trials were conducted, on the basis of which this vaccine has been developed. Question - What do WHO and other international institutions say about this vaccine? Answer - See in detailed pointers- WHO has supported the use of QDENGA in countries and regions where dengue cases are high. According to WHO, pre-vaccination screening for dengue is not necessary for every individual in such regions. QDENGA has also received WHO pre-qualification. This certification indicates that the vaccine meets the established international standards for quality, safety, and efficacy. After receiving WHO pre-qualification, international agencies like UNICEF and PAHO (Pan American Health Organization) can also purchase this vaccine. How does QDENGA (TAK-003) work? The function of any vaccine is to prepare the body to fight disease. QDENGA works in a similar way. Understand with pointers- QDENGA uses a weakened (live attenuated) form of the dengue virus. This virus does not cause illness, but it activates the body's immune system. After vaccination, the body develops antibodies and immune memory. Later, if the dengue virus enters the body, the immune system quickly recognizes it and tries to fight it. This helps reduce the impact of the infection. Question - Who can get this vaccine? Answer - In India, the QDENGA vaccine has been approved for people aged 4 to 60 years. This vaccine can be taken by both those who have had dengue before and those who have never had it. Question - How does this vaccine build immunity in the body? After receiving QDENGA, several biological processes begin in the body, which help develop protection against the dengue virus. After receiving the vaccine, the body starts producing antibodies against the dengue virus. Immune memory cells are also prepared, which help recognize the virus for a long time. When the dengue virus enters the body, these antibodies and memory cells become active and help fight the virus. Does this vaccine provide 100% protection? No, the QDENGA vaccine does not guarantee 100% protection. During trials, two doses of QDENGA reduced the risk of hospitalization due to dengue by 90% after 18 months. After 4.5 years, this risk remained reduced by 84%. Therefore, even after getting vaccinated, it is important to take measures to protect against mosquitoes. How many years of protection does the vaccine provide? According to clinical trial data, the vaccine's effect has been observed for at least 4.5 years. During this period, vaccinated individuals were continuously tracked. If someone has already had dengue, can they still get this vaccine? Yes, this vaccine can be administered in both situations, whether one has had dengue before or never had dengue. Who should not get this vaccine? QDENGA is not suitable for some people. Such people should definitely consult a doctor before getting vaccinated. See in the graphic- How many total doses of this vaccine will be administered? The complete vaccination of QDENGA will be completed in two doses. There will be an interval of approximately 3 months between both doses. Will a booster dose be needed in the future? Currently, a booster dose is not recommended for QDENGA. Does it have any side effects? Yes, like other vaccines, it can also have some mild and temporary side effects. These usually resolve on their own in 13 days. See in the graphic- What symptoms should prompt an immediate doctor's visit after vaccination? If severe symptoms appear after vaccination, it is important to consult a doctor immediately. See all severe symptoms in the graphic- If another vaccine has been administered recently, what should be the interval? It depends on which other vaccine it is. A uniform interval is not set for all vaccines. Therefore, before getting QDENGA, be sure to inform your doctor about your vaccination history. . More news... 班加罗尔医生称赞登革热 疫苗 是公共卫生领域的重大进步 - 德干先驱报

Bengaluru doctors hail dengue vaccine as significant advance in public health - Deccan Herald
新闻简报班加罗尔医生对印度政府批准 Qdenga(一种暴露前登革热 疫苗)表示欢迎,认为这是一个重要的公共卫生里程碑。疫苗 经印度药品监管总局批准,在 疫苗接种 后的第一年中,在预防确诊登革热方面显示出 80% 的功效,在预防住院方面有 90% 的功效,并在长达 4.5 年的住院治疗方面提供 84% 的保护。卡纳塔克邦在 7 月第三周报告了 229 例登革热病例,凸显了持续的负担。建议登革热流行地区 4 至 60 岁的个体使用 疫苗,分两剂注射,间隔三个月。医生强调,虽然 疫苗 可以减轻严重程度,但它并不能提供完整的保护,强调需要继续采取蚊子控制和公众意识措施。

时间
发布于 2026-07-25。证据中未指定事件日期。
地点与对象
印度卡纳塔克邦班加罗尔。受影响人群包括居住在登革热流行地区或前往登革热流行地区的 4 至 60 岁人群。

印度药品监管总局 (DCGI) 批准了 Qdenga,一种暴露前登革热 疫苗。班加罗尔的医生对这一批准表示欢迎,认为这是一个重要的公共卫生里程碑。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bengaluru doctors hail dengue vaccine as significant advance in public health - Deccan Herald

News Brief
Bengaluru doctors have welcomed the Indian government's approval of Qdenga, a pre-exposure dengue vaccine, as a significant public health milestone. The vaccine, approved by the Drugs Controller General of India, has shown 80% efficacy in preventing confirmed dengue and 90% efficacy in preventing hospitalisation in the first year post-vaccination, with 84% protection against hospitalisation lasting up to 4.5 years. Karnataka reported 229 dengue cases in the third week of July, highlighting the ongoing burden. The vaccine is recommended for individuals aged 4 to 60 in dengue-endemic areas, administered in two doses three months apart. Doctors stress that while the vaccine reduces severity, it does not provide complete protection, emphasizing the need for continued mosquito control and public awareness measures.
Original source text (English)
Representative picture of a dengue-spreading mosquito. Credit: iStock Images Bengaluru: The Drugs Controller General of India (DCGI) has approved Qdenga, a pre-exposure dengue vaccine designed to reduce the risk of infection, severe disease and hospitalisation. Experience a more refined e-paper today Doctors in the city have welcomed the approval, calling it a significant public health milestone that could help reduce the burden of dengue, particularly during peak transmission seasons. Weekly dengue cases in Karnataka climbed from 93 to 127 in the first week of July, fell to 106 in the second week and surged to 229 in the third, data from the Health and Family Welfare Department showed. Dr G Nasiruddin, Consultant, Internal Medicine, said, "Dengue continues to be a huge burden to the healthcare system every monsoon, with recurrent outbreaks resulting in hospitalisation and death in severe cases. The vaccine, when introduced through a well-planned immunisation strategy, has the potential to reduce the number of symptomatic infections, severe dengue cases and hospital admissions particularly in high-risk regions." A successful dengue control programme would require vaccination combined with sustained mosquito-control measures, community participation and public awareness, he added. Qdenga is administered in two subcutaneous injections three months apart. The vaccine is recommended for all, except pregnant and breastfeeding women, doctors said. It is advised for people aged 4 to 60 living in or travelling to dengue-endemic areas. Dr Priyanka Prakash, Associate Consultant, Infectious Diseases, said, "Qdenga showed about 80% efficacy in preventing confirmed dengue and 90% efficacy in preventing hospitalisation due to dengue in the first year after vaccination. Protection against hospitalisation remained strong (84%) through 4.5 years, though protection is weaker against Serotypes 3 and 4 in people never previously infected." On vaccination in children, Dr Sandeep S Reddy, Consultant, Infectious Diseases, said, "It will not be given to children with weakened immune systems or those taking high-dose steroids. While a blood test for prior dengue infection is not strictly required with the newly launched vaccine, doctors have to consider the medical history of the child." The approval has also raised questions about the vaccine's effectiveness after a dengue diagnosis. Dr AN Venkatesh, Senior Consultant and Head, Emergency Medicine, said, "The vaccine helps reduce severity but does not 100% protect from infection. Hence, people should continue to take precautions against mosquitoes. If someone has fever or is feeling unwell, they should seek prompt medical advice."

Doctors emphasized the need for vaccination combined with mosquito-control measures, community participation, and public awareness. The measures are due to take effect on July 24 as the temporary 10 percent worldwide tariffs expire. USTR Jamieson Greer said the move is aimed at combating forced labor in global supply chains, describing how the U.S. has long banned imports of goods made with forced labor and urging its trading partners to effectively enforce similar restrictions. “The action will tackle both human rights abuses and distortive trade practices to improve workers’ welfare,” Greer said. He also welcomed trading partners that have swiftly adopted forced-labor import bans and said he looks forward to ensuring their effective enforcement. In a Bloomberg interview broadcast on July 24, Commerce Minister Cham Nimul said Cambodia has taken additional measures with three ministries to enhance joint efforts in preventing the import of goods linked to forced labor. Nimul said Cambodia is also negotiating a quota system with the U.S., along with other trading partners such as Bangladesh, Indonesia, and Malaysia. The system would allow Cambodia to use U.S.-sourced raw materials to produce garments and export them back to the U.S. with quota-free or duty-free treatment. Meanwhile, Deputy Prime Minister Sun Chanthol said in an interview on the same day that he was pleased with the 10 percent tariff rate, which falls within the 10 percent to 12.5 percent range. However, Cambodia is awaiting the second component, excess capacity, which it expects will not exceed 9 percent. Among the 60 economies investigated, a 10 percent tariff will apply to economies that have imposed a forced-labor import prohibition, committed to adopt such a prohibition through an Agreement on Reciprocal Trade, and have partial measures to restrict imports of goods linked to forced labor. In ASEAN, Cambodia, Indonesia, and Malaysia face a 10 percent tariff, while Vietnam, Thailand, Singapore, and the Philippines are among the economies that will be subject to a new 12.5 percent tariff. The USTR also approved exemptions for certain products, including essential raw materials whose tariffs could affect domestic supply, products that could trigger economic disruptions, and products that cannot be produced or sourced affordably domestically or elsewhere. Additionally, the exemptions also apply to selected products from countries such as Cambodia that could encourage stronger action against forced-labor imports. On July 1, the Ministry of Commerce, the Ministry of Economy and Finance, and the Ministry of Labor and Vocational Training issued a Joint Prakas establishing a regulatory framework prohibiting the import of goods linked to forced labor. Door Remains Open For Tariff Exemption, but Textile Gaps Pose Risks Cambodia’s garment sector is among the products that will be exempted from the new tariff. While this would present opportunities, former American Chamber of Commerce in Cambodia (AmCham) executive director Cassey Barnett said it could pose risks as well. Barnett said the special tariff exemption is based on the volume of the U.S.-sourced cotton or cotton fabric imported by Cambodia, which aims to enhance U.S. production and reduce reliance on China. In 2025, Cambodia exported $4 billion worth of garments to the U.S. During the same year, Cambodia imported $2.8 billion worth of cotton and fabrics from China, which were used to produce garments exported to the U.S. and the European Union, Barnett wrote on his Facebook page on July 24. In contrast, Cambodia’s imports of cotton and fabrics from the U.S. recorded a small figure, worth only $137,000. In the short term, Barnett said Cambodia could lose orders to Indonesia and Bangladesh, which also face a 10 percent U.S. tariff along with garment tariff exemptions. “These two countries are Cambodia’s competitors, which have large textile manufacturing industries, while Cambodia still lacks,” Barnett said. To prevent the gradual loss of orders, he advised Cambodia to act swiftly to attract investment in textile manufacturing. He also highlighted other challenges for Cambodia, including high electricity costs and difficult environmental impact assessment procedures. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

DCGI 批准 Qdenga 登革热 疫苗,医生欢呼重大公共卫生促进 - Daijiworld

DCGI approves Qdenga dengue vaccine, doctors hail major public health boost - Daijiworld
新闻简报印度药品监管总局 (DCGI) 已批准 Qdenga,一种登革热 疫苗,旨在降低感染风险、严重疾病和住院治疗。这一批准是在卡纳塔克邦报告登革热病例不断增加之际发布的,7 月份的三周内病例数从 93 例增加到 229 例。疫苗 分两次给药,间隔三个月,建议流行地区 4-60 岁的个体使用,但不包括孕妇、哺乳期母亲和免疫系统较弱的人。临床研究表明,第一年预防实验室确诊登革热的有效性约为 80%,预防住院的有效性为 90%,且保护效果可持续长达 4.5 年。医疗保健专家强调,疫苗接种 应补充现有的蚊子控制措施和公众意识活动,因为 疫苗 不能提供全面的感染保护。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 26 日。证据中未明确说明事件日期。
地点与对象
印度,特别是卡纳塔克邦,该邦报告的登革热病例持续波动。建议居住在登革热流行地区或前往登革热流行地区的 4-60 岁人士使用 疫苗。
事件
印度药品监管总局 (DCGI) 已批准 Qdenga,一种登革热 疫苗,旨在降低感染、严重疾病和住院的风险。医疗专家称这是印度抗击蚊媒疾病的重要一步。
规模、影响与风险
卡纳塔克邦报告登革热病例从 7 月第一周的 93 例增加到第二周的 129 例,然后急剧上升到第三周的 229 例。季风期间,登革热给医疗保健系统带来沉重负担,季节性 暴发 会导致严重疾病和住院人数增加。
应对、状态与不确定性
疫苗 分两次皮下注射,间隔三个月。不建议孕妇或哺乳期妇女、免疫系统较弱的儿童或接受大剂量类固醇 治疗 的患者使用。虽然不强制要求对既往登革热感染进行血液检测,但建议医生在 疫苗接种 之前仔细评估儿童的病史。疫苗 有助于减轻登革热的严重程度,但不能提供完整的保护,因此应继续采取预防蚊虫叮咬的措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

DCGI approves Qdenga dengue vaccine, doctors hail major public health boost - Daijiworld

News Brief
India's Drugs Controller General of India (DCGI) has approved Qdenga, a dengue vaccine aimed at reducing infection risk, severe illness, and hospitalization. This approval comes as Karnataka reports rising dengue cases, with numbers increasing from 93 to 229 over three weeks in July. The vaccine, administered in two doses three months apart, is recommended for individuals aged 4-60 in endemic regions but excludes pregnant women, breastfeeding mothers, and those with weakened immune systems. Clinical studies show approximately 80% effectiveness in preventing laboratory-confirmed dengue and 90% in preventing hospitalizations in the first year, with protection remaining high for up to 4.5 years. Healthcare experts emphasize that vaccination should complement existing mosquito control measures and public awareness campaigns, as the vaccine does not provide complete protection against infection.

Home News Obituary Recipes Charity Special ಕನ್ನಡ Live TV RADIO Advertise Contact Us Matrimonial Properties Jobs General Ads Red Chillies Music Ask Dr Greetings Astrology Tribute Of Love Latest India poised to gain from global biopharma patent expiry boom Wed, Jul 29 Brain changes may be linked to sleep disorders, new study suggests Wed, Jul 29 Monsoon health: Vitamin C-rich foods can help strengthen immunity during rainy season Wed, Jul 29 Study finds heart has its own neural network that helps regulate heartbeat Wed, Jul 29 Uganda declares end of Ebola outbreak Tue, Jul 28 HPV vaccine can help eliminate cervical cancer in India: Dattatreyudu Nori Tue, Jul 28 Weekend binge drinking can silently damage liver, warn doctors Tue, Jul 28 Health DCGI approves Qdenga dengue vaccine, doctors hail major public health boost Sun, Jul 26 2026 12:32:13 PM Discover moreMachine Learning & Artificial IntelligenceHinduismNews Daijiworld Media Network - Bengaluru Bengaluru, Jul 26: The Drugs Controller General of India (DCGI) has approved Qdenga, a dengue vaccine designed to reduce the risk of infection, severe illness and hospitalisation, with healthcare experts describing the move as a significant step forward in the country's fight against the mosquito-borne disease. The approval comes as Karnataka continues to report fluctuating dengue cases. According to data from the state's Health and Family Welfare Department, reported cases increased from 93 in the first week of July to 129 in the second week before rising sharply to 229 in the third week. Dr G Nasiruddin, consultant in internal medicine, said dengue continues to place a heavy burden on healthcare systems during the monsoon, with seasonal outbreaks often leading to severe illness and increased hospital admissions. "The vaccine, when introduced through a well-structured immunisation strategy, has the potential to significantly reduce symptomatic cases, severe dengue and hospitalisations, particularly in high-risk regions," he said. He added that vaccination should complement existing mosquito control measures, community participation and public awareness campaigns for effective dengue prevention. Qdenga is administered as two subcutaneous doses given three months apart. Doctors recommend the vaccine for individuals aged between four and 60 years who live in or travel to dengue-endemic regions. However, it is not recommended for pregnant or breastfeeding women. Dr Priyanka Prakash, associate consultant in infectious diseases, said clinical studies showed the vaccine was around 80 per cent effective in preventing laboratory-confirmed dengue and approximately 90 per cent effective in preventing dengue-related hospitalisation during the first year after vaccination. She added that protection against hospitalisation remained high at 84 per cent for up to 4.5 years, although its effectiveness against dengue virus serotypes 3 and 4 was lower among individuals with no previous dengue infection. Dr Sandeep S Reddy, consultant in infectious diseases, said the vaccine would not be administered to children with weakened immune systems or those receiving high-dose steroid treatment. While prior blood testing for previous dengue infection is not mandatory, he advised doctors to carefully evaluate a child's medical history before vaccination. Dr A N Venkatesh, senior consultant and head of emergency medicine, said the vaccine helps reduce the severity of dengue but does not provide complete protection against infection. He stressed that preventive measures against mosquito bites should continue even after vaccination and urged people experiencing fever or symptoms suggestive of dengue to seek medical attention without delay. 印度批准首个针对 4 至 60 岁人群的登革热 疫苗 - Devdiscourse

India Approves First Dengue Vaccine for People Aged 4 to 60 - Devdiscourse
新闻简报印度已批准其首个登革热 疫苗 Qdenga®,适用于 4 至 60 岁的个体,这标志着该国抗击登革热的一个重要里程碑。疫苗由德国Takeda GmbH生产,由Takeda Biopharmaceuticals India Pvt.进口,已获得卫生和家庭福利部下属的中央药品标准控制组织(CDSCO)的上市许可。Qdenga® 是一种活的减毒四价 疫苗,旨在预防所有四种 登革病毒 血清型,分两次 0.5 毫升剂量给药,间隔三个月。该批准得到了全球和印度临床研究的支持,疫苗 已在 42 个国家获得批准,并通过了世界卫生组织的资格预审。卫生当局希望这一新工具能够补充现有的登革热控制措施,包括国家 监测 传染病控制计划下的蚊子控制和疾病 监测。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

India Approves First Dengue Vaccine for People Aged 4 to 60 - Devdiscourse

News Brief
India has approved its first dengue vaccine, Qdenga®, for individuals aged 4 to 60 years, marking a significant milestone in the country's fight against dengue. The vaccine, manufactured by Takeda GmbH, Germany, and imported by Takeda Biopharmaceuticals India Pvt., received marketing authorisation from the Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO) under the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. Qdenga® is a live, attenuated tetravalent vaccine designed to protect against all four dengue virus serotypes, administered as two 0.5 ml doses three months apart. The approval is backed by global and Indian clinical studies, with the vaccine already cleared in 42 countries and prequalified by the WHO. Health authorities expect this new tool to complement existing dengue control measures, including mosquito control and disease surveillance, under the National Vector Borne Disease Control Programme.
Original source text (English)
About Career Advertisement Media Partnership Contact Us HOME NEWS RESEARCH LIVE DISCOURSE PERSPECTIVES AND INSIGHTS SUBMIT PRESS RELEASE NEWS DESK RESEARCH LIVE DISCOURSE PERSPECTIVES AND INSIGHTS INTERVIEW Agro-Forestry Art & Culture Technology Economy Education Energy Politics Law & Governance Health Science Social Sports Transport Urban Development WASH Home News Health Article India Approves First Dengue Vaccine for People Aged 4 to 60 Devdiscourse News Desk | New Delhi | Updated: 25-07-2026 14:40 IST | Created: 25-07-2026 14:40 IST Image Credit: Credit: ChatGPT Country: India SHARE Key Takeaways AI Summary Analyzing article... India has approved its first vaccine for the prevention of dengue, marking a major milestone in the country's fight against one of its most widespread mosquito-borne diseases. The Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO), under the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, has granted marketing authorisation to Qdenga®, a tetravalent dengue vaccine manufactured by Takeda GmbH, Germany, and to be imported by Takeda Biopharmaceuticals India Pvt. Ltd. The approval follows a detailed scientific review of the vaccine's quality, safety and effectiveness under the provisions of the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, paving the way for a new preventive tool alongside existing dengue control measures. Vaccine designed to protect against all four dengue virus types Qdenga® is a live, attenuated tetravalent dengue vaccine developed using recombinant DNA technology. It is designed to protect against all four dengue virus serotypes by using a dengue virus type-2 backbone engineered with serotype-specific surface proteins. The vaccine is supplied as a freeze-dried powder that is reconstituted before administration and is given as a subcutaneous injection. It has been approved for individuals aged 4 to 60 years, with the recommended schedule consisting of two 0.5 ml doses administered three months apart. Approval backed by global and Indian clinical studies The regulatory approval is supported by an extensive international clinical development programme that evaluated the vaccine's safety, immune response and effectiveness in both dengue-endemic and non-endemic regions. Clinical trials included participants from more than eight countries, including Brazil, Thailand and Sri Lanka, while a key Phase III study was also conducted in India involving volunteers between 4 and 60 years of age. These studies formed an important part of the evidence reviewed before approving. The vaccine has already received regulatory clearance in 42 countries, including members of the European Union, the United Kingdom, Switzerland, Indonesia, Malaysia and Thailand. It has also received World Health Organization (WHO) prequalification, and more than 24 million doses have been distributed globally. Post-marketing safety data from countries using the vaccine have not identified any significant safety concerns. New vaccine adds to India's dengue control strategy Health authorities said the approval is expected to strengthen India's overall dengue prevention efforts by complementing existing measures such as mosquito control, disease surveillance, early diagnosis and clinical management under the National Vector Borne Disease Control Programme. The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare said it remains committed to ensuring timely access to safe, effective and quality-assured vaccines through a science-based regulatory system. The approval of Qdenga® represents an important addition to India's public health response as the country continues to tackle the growing burden of dengue. ICMR 研究发现所有 4 种登革热类型均在流行,DENV-2 占主导地位

ICMR study finds all 4 dengue types circulating, DENV-2 dominant
新闻简报ICMR 研究显示,所有四种类型的 登革病毒(DENV-1 至 DENV-4)均在印度各地流行,其中 DENV-2 是主要菌株,占最近病例的一半以上。该研究分析了来自 25 个州和中央直辖区的 6,889 个确诊样本,还发现大约 7% 的患者同时感染多种登革热类型,导致血小板减少、出血和关节疼痛等更严重的症状。尽管已在印度获得批准的登革热 疫苗,但某些地区存在所有四种血清型(被描述为高流行),使 暴发 控制工作和 疫苗 开发变得复杂。该研究强调了在多种流行病毒类型地区管理登革热的挑战。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 28 日。证据中未指定事件时间表。
地点与对象
印度,横跨 25 个邦和中央直辖区。证据中未具体说明受影响的人群。
事件
根据 ICMR 的一项研究,所有四种类型的 登革病毒(DENV-1 至 DENV-4)均在印度流行,其中 DENV-2 占主导地位,导致最近一半以上的病例。该研究分析了 6,889 个已确认的样本。
规模、影响与风险
大约 7% 的患者同时感染一种以上登革热类型,导致更严重的症状,如血小板低、出血问题和关节疼痛。所有四种血清型在某些地区(高流行)的传播使 暴发 的控制和 疫苗 的发展变得复杂。经批准的登革热 疫苗 目前在印度上市。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究深入了解当前在印度流行的 登革病毒 类型及其主导地位。然而,证据并未具体说明正在进行的应对措施、调查状态或未解决的信息。来源为部分原始报告正文。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

ICMR study finds all 4 dengue types circulating, DENV-2 dominant

News Brief
An ICMR study reveals that all four types of dengue virus (DENV-1 to DENV-4) are circulating across India, with DENV-2 being the dominant strain, responsible for over half of recent cases. The research, which analyzed 6,889 confirmed samples from 25 states and union territories, also found that approximately 7% of patients had co-infections with multiple dengue types, leading to more severe symptoms like low platelets, bleeding, and joint pain. The presence of all four serotypes in some regions, described as hyperendemic, complicates outbreak control efforts and vaccine development, though an approved dengue vaccine is available in India. The study highlights the challenges in managing dengue in regions with multiple circulating virus types.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

研究揭示 登革病毒 感染旨在对抗疾病的免疫细胞

Study Reveals Dengue Virus Infects Immune Cells Meant to Fight Disease
新闻简报A groundbreaking study published in the Journal of Virology on July 20, 2026, reveals that dengue virus can infect and replicate inside memory B cells, immune cells typically responsible for protecting the body from infection. Conducted by researchers at the University of Vermont and SUNY Upstate Medical University, the discovery uncovers a previously unrecognized mechanism by which the virus evades immune defenses. The study shows that dengue-specific memory B cells can become infected by all four dengue virus types and produce infectious virus, potentially explaining why subsequent infections with different strains can be more severe. This research opens new avenues for understanding dengue disease and developing interventions, including therapies targeting B cells, which are already approved for other conditions. With dengue threatening 40 percent of the world's population and infecting an estimated 100 million people annually, these findings could lead to novel treatments for a disease with no specific approved drugs.
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 28 日;该研究于 2026 年 7 月 20 日发表在《病毒学杂志》上。
地点与对象
佛蒙特大学 (UVM) 罗伯特·拉纳医学博士学院和纽约州北部州立大学 (SUNY Upstate) 纽约州锡拉丘兹医科大学的研究人员;影响存在携带登革热蚊子的地区的人口,包括美国南部部分地区。
事件
《病毒学杂志》上发表的一项研究表明,登革病毒 可以在记忆 B 细胞(传统上用于对抗疾病的免疫细胞)内感染和复制,代表了病毒逃避免疫防御的一种以前未被认识的方式。
规模、影响与风险
Four different dengue viruses (DENV1–4) infect an estimated 100 million people each year worldwide; more than four billion people live in regions where dengue-carrying mosquitoes are present; symptoms can include high fever, severe headaches, muscle and bone pain, and in severe cases, life-threatening complications.
应对、状态与不确定性
这项研究是由 Sean Diehl 博士和 Adam Waickman 博士实验室的研究人员进行的;研究结果指出了治疗登革热的新策略,包括针对 B 细胞的潜在疗法;尚无专门针对登革病毒的获批药物,疫苗面临挑战;未来的策略可能包括在初次登革热感染后选择性消除或修改高风险记忆 B 细胞的疗法。

Original source text (English)
show the image caption close caption Gaby Madrigal, a Cellular, Molecular, and Biomedical Science graduate student at the University of Vermont, works in the Sean Diehl, Ph.D., Laboratory and is first author on a recently published study that reveals how dengue virus evades immune defenses. (photo: Andy Duback) Study Reveals Dengue Virus Infects Immune Cells Meant to Fight Disease Discovery points to potential new approaches for treating dengue Health & Medicine Science & Technology Student Success University News July 28, 2026 By Stephanie Knific and Janet Essman Franz Researchers at the University of Vermont (UVM) Robert Larner, M.D. College of Medicine and State University of New York Upstate (SUNY Upstate) Medical University in Syracuse, New York, have discovered that dengue virus can infect and replicate inside memory B cells, immune cells traditionally understood to help protect the body from infection. The findings, published July 20 in the Journal of Virology , reveal a previously unrecognized way the virus may evade immune defenses and point toward new strategies for treating a disease that threatens 40 percent of the world’s population, including parts of the southern United States. The study was conducted by researchers in the laboratories of Sean Diehl, Ph.D., professor of microbiology and molecular genetics at UVM, and Adam Waickman, Ph.D., associate professor of microbiology and immunology at SUNY Upstate. Four different dengue viruses (DENV1–4) are transmitted by tropical mosquitoes, infecting an estimated 100 million people each year worldwide. More than four billion people live in regions where dengue-carrying mosquitoes are present. Symptoms can include high fever, severe headaches, muscle and bone pain, and, in severe cases, life-threatening complications caused by damage to blood vessels and dangerously low blood pressure. Scientists have long known that a previous dengue infection can sometimes make a later infection with a different dengue virus strain more severe, a phenomenon known as antibody-dependent enhancement (ADE). In ADE, antibodies generated during a first infection bind to a second dengue strain but fail to neutralize it, instead helping the virus enter certain immune cells. “It is a high bar to show something genuinely new in dengue research ... it opens new avenues for understanding dengue disease and developing interventions to prevent or treat it.” — Sean Diehl, Ph.D. Previous work from the Diehl and Waickman laboratories identified another possible pathway. The researchers showed that dengue virus could enter B cells—the cells that make antibodies—through the B cell receptor, a molecule on the cell surface responsible for recognizing pathogens. The team named this process B cell receptor-dependent enhancement (BDE). In the new study, co-led by UVM cellular, molecular, and biomedical sciences graduate student Gaby Madrigal and SUNY Upstate staff scientist and recent Ph.D. graduate Chad Gebo, the team demonstrated for the first time that dengue-specific memory B cells can become infected by all four dengue virus types and produce infectious virus. Memory B cells are generated following an initial infection and persist for years, making them likely targets during subsequent exposure to a different dengue strain. Photographs taken through a high-resolution imaging microscope at 15-minute intervals show dengue viruses (yellow dots) contacting a dengue-virus-sensing B cell (blue and green regions) and, over the course of 45 minutes at body temperature, gaining access to an internal compartment called the endosome (purple area), which allows the virus to begin replicating inside the B cell. (photo: Sean Diehl Laboratory) Using advanced microscopy techniques, in collaboration with Menelaos Symeonides, Ph.D., assistant professor of microbiology and molecular genetics at UVM, the team was able to visualize dengue virus entering these cells and to identify potential vulnerabilities in the process by targeting molecular signaling pathways activated by the B cell receptor that are required for infection. “It is a high bar to show something genuinely new in dengue research and I am very proud of this high-quality data produced by our trainees who rose to the occasion,” said Dr. Diehl. The findings could have important therapeutic implications. There are currently no approved drugs that specifically target dengue virus, and vaccines continue to face challenges related to broad implementation and effectiveness across different populations. In contrast, several drugs that target B cells are already approved for treating cancers and autoimmune diseases, raising the possibility that similar approaches could be adapted for dengue treatment. Researchers say future strategies might also include therapies designed to selectively eliminate or modify high-risk memory B cells after an initial dengue infection, potentially reducing the risk of severe disease following later exposure. “This work challenges existing assumptions about how dengue interacts with the immune system,” said Diehl. “By revealing a previously unrecognized route of infection, it opens new avenues for understanding dengue disease and developing interventions to prevent or treat it.” The researchers believe the discovery represents an important advance in understanding how dengue virus subverts immune defenses and may help guide the development of future therapies aimed at reducing the global burden of the disease. Read the study published in the Journal of Virology Media Contact Stephanie Knific Executive Director of Strategic Communications and Marketing Office of Medical Communications stephanie.knific@med.uvm.edu +1 802-656-0729

印度批准首个登革热 疫苗 - The Rising尼泊尔

India approves first dengue vaccine - The Rising Nepal
新闻简报印度已批准其首个登革热药物 疫苗 Qdenga,由武田开发,适用于 4 至 60 岁的个体。中央药物标准控制组织 (CDSCO) 在审查 疫苗 的质量、安全性和有效性后批准了该药物。Qdenga 间隔三个月注射两次,可预防所有四种 登革病毒 血清型,并已在 42 个国家/地区获得批准。自 2021 年以来,印度报告了超过 100 万登革热病例和 1,500 人死亡,该国也在开发自己的单剂量 疫苗,DengiAll,目前处于后期临床试验阶段。该批准标志着抗击登革热迈出了重要一步,登革热对世界近一半人口构成威胁。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026-07-26。事件日期:2026年7月21日,星期二。
地点与对象
受影响人群:4岁至60岁的人群。
事件
印度已批准其首个登革热 疫苗 Qdenga,由日本制药商武田 (Takeda) 开发。印度中央药品标准控制组织(CDSCO)在审查其质量、安全性和有效性后批准了该产品。
规模、影响与风险
The vaccine protects against all four dengue virus serotypes. Since 2021, India has reported more than one million dengue cases and at least 1,500 deaths. The vaccine is approved in 42 countries, with over 24 million doses distributed worldwide and no major safety concerns reported.
应对、状态与不确定性
疫苗 分两次注射,间隔三个月。武田与印度 疫苗 制造商 Biological E 合作进行制造。印度还在开发自己的单剂量登革热药物疫苗,即DengiAll,目前正处于临床试验的最后阶段。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

India approves first dengue vaccine - The Rising Nepal

News Brief
India has approved its first dengue vaccine, Qdenga, developed by Takeda, for individuals aged 4 to 60. The Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO) granted approval after reviewing the vaccine's quality, safety, and efficacy. Qdenga, administered as two injections three months apart, protects against all four dengue virus serotypes and is already approved in 42 countries. India, which has reported over one million dengue cases and 1,500 deaths since 2021, is also developing its own single-dose vaccine, DengiAll, currently in late-stage clinical trials. The approval marks a significant step in combating dengue, which poses a risk to nearly half the world's population.

2026 NEW DELHI, July 21 : India has approved its first dengue vaccine, authorising the use of Japanese drugmaker Takeda's Qdenga jab, the health ministry said on Tuesday.Dengue, which causes severe flu-like symptoms and debilitating body aches, has exploded globally, fuelled by rising temperatures and densely populated cities.India's Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO) approved the vaccine for people between the ages of four and 60 after reviewing its quality, safety and efficacy, the ministry said."The vaccine is indicated for the prevention of dengue disease in individuals 4 to 60 years of age," it said in a statement.Qdenga is given as two injections, three months apart.Takeda has a collaboration with Indian vaccine maker Biological E, aimed at expanding the drug's manufacturing.The vaccine protects against all four dengue virus serotypes and is approved in 42 countries, including the European Union, the United Kingdom, Switzerland and Indonesia.It has also received World Health Organization (WHO) prequalification.More than 24 million doses have been distributed worldwide, with no major safety concerns reported so far, the ministry said.Dengue has spread rapidly in recent decades due to urbanisation, climate change and changing weather patterns.According to the WHO, nearly half the world's population is at risk, with an estimated 100 million to 400 million infections each year.Since 2021, India has reported more than one million dengue cases and at least 1,500 deaths, according to the latest official data.India is also set to get its own dengue vaccine with Panacea Biotec's candidate, DengiAll, having entered the final stage of clinical trials.Once approved, it would become one of the world's first single-dose dengue vaccines. 研究表明 登革病毒 会感染旨在预防感染的免疫细胞 - Bioengineer.org

Study Shows Dengue Virus Infects Immune Cells Designed to Prevent Infection - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报Bioengineer.org 于 2026 年 7 月 27 日发表的一项研究发现,登革病毒 会感染通常旨在预防感染的免疫细胞。这一在科学背景下报告的发现凸显了对 登革病毒 如何与免疫系统相互作用的新理解。该研究没有提供有关病例数、死亡人数或受影响地点的具体细节,也没有提及任何公共卫生应对措施或调查状态。该研究重点关注 登革病毒 的生物学机制及其对 免疫应答 的影响,但没有表明任何直接的健康风险或操作影响。这些发现基于科学分析,并不表明当前存在 暴发 或流行病。该研究是对病毒与免疫系统相互作用的更广泛探索的一部分,为病毒学和免疫学领域做出了贡献。
